A step whose live path already holds its new state does nothing and removes what it staged, since no later step will publish it and no journal will name it again: two configurations sharing one skills directory plan two publishes of one path, and the second of them is that step. A staging directory left by a process that stopped before its journal existed is swept by the next command that stages into that same directory (an install, a placement or an update), under the lock and after recovery; retained content is never swept. A command that refuses after staging content and before its journal exists removes what it staged, for the same reason: no journal names it, so the sweep of a later command into those same directories would be the only thing that ever could, and a copy staged in a client directory no later install targets would stay for good.

The journal cannot run git, so a command that mutates agentx home hands it what its `ref` and `worktree` steps need: reading the value of every ref it names, moving the refs of each phase in one transaction with their expected old values, and adding, aligning and removing a fork's worktree. Every command that writes has git, since git is the startup gate for all of them.

A `worktree` step is decided from files, with no git: the path is the worktree when its `.git` file names an admin directory of the account repo whose `gitdir` names the path back and whose `HEAD` is the branch, either pointer absolute or relative. A path that holds nothing but a `.git` file whose registration is gone or not yet on the branch is what a `worktree add` stopped part way leaves, since git writes the admin directory and the `.git` file before `HEAD` names the branch and cleans them up only after a signal it can catch: recovery adds the worktree over it. A path that is absent or an empty directory gets the worktree; anything else refuses, as any step that finds neither state does. A `worktree` step that names entries in `beside` then checks out each of them that is absent from the worktree's root, from the index it has just aligned, and leaves one that is there as it is; recovery does the same, so a run stopped between the add and the checkout loses nothing. A `worktree` step whose git fails is exit code 8 (`account_repo`), as a `ref` step's is.

A journal whose first transaction of refs is refused because a ref no longer holds the value the command read under the lock has changed nothing, since that transaction runs before every other step: the journal and the content it staged are removed at once rather than left for a recovery that would refuse them every time, and the command is exit code 6 (`refused`) saying that the skill changed while the command ran, so that nothing was changed, with the hint to run it again. Git run by a user or an agent in a fork's worktree moves the fork's branch without agentx's lock, which is how this happens.

Under the exclusive lock the command writes the new content to `mutations/<id>.staged`, then the journal `mutations/<id>.json`, each by temp file, fsync, rename and directory fsync. Only then does it apply the steps, rename the staged files over the live files, rewrite the journal with `progress` `applied` and remove it. `<id>` is the Unix time in nanoseconds, `-` and four random bytes in hex, so journals sort oldest first. The journal is one JSON object:

| Field | Meaning |
|---|---|
| `progress` | `staged` until every live file is replaced, then `applied` |
| `replace` | the live files the mutation replaces, one entry each |
| `replace[].path` | the live file's absolute path |
| `replace[].old` | SHA-256 hex of the live file before the mutation, or `absent` when there was no file |
| `replace[].new` | SHA-256 hex of the staged content |
| `replace[].staged` | the staged file, next to the journal |
| `steps` | the changes outside the state files, in the order they are recorded; a `ref` step that creates or moves a ref is applied before every path step and one that deletes a ref after them and after the state files; absent when the mutation has none |
| `steps[].kind` | `ref`, `publish`, `link`, `remove`, `worktree` or `move` |
| `steps[].path` | the live path of a `publish`, `link`, `remove`, `worktree` or `move`, which for a `move` is where the directory goes |
| `steps[].from` | the live directory a `move` takes away |
| `steps[].git_dir`, `steps[].ref` | the repository and ref of a `ref` step; the repository of a `worktree` step |
| `steps[].old` | what the step expects to find: for a path `absent`, `link:<target>`, `dir:<fingerprint>` or `other`; for a ref its object id, empty for a ref that must not exist |
| `steps[].new` | what the step leaves behind, in the same words; empty on a `ref` step deletes that ref; `worktree:<branch>` on a `worktree` step, the short name of the branch, such as `skills/pdf` |
| `steps[].staged` | the directory a `publish` renames into place |
| `steps[].retained` | where a `remove` keeps the content it displaced |
| `steps[].beside` | the entries a `worktree` step checks out at the worktree's root when they are absent: what the branch tip holds there beside the skill directory; absent when there are none |

The fingerprint of a directory is SHA-256 over every entry below it in bytewise order of its relative path: the path, `NUL`, a kind and a length, `NUL`, then the file's bytes or the symlink's target. It is not the [content hash](#content-hash), which names a version of a skill: it names the bytes on disk, so that a directory rewritten by hand is never mistaken for the one the mutation captured.

Recovery runs under the exclusive lock and decides from what the live path holds, not from `progress`, since a process can stop after a write and before recording it. A step whose live path holds its `new` state is done; one that holds `old` is applied; one that holds neither was changed by something else, and both versions are kept: the journal stays and the command fails with exit code 6 (`refused`) naming the path. A live file whose hash is `new` is done: the journal, and a staged file still present, are removed and `version` is not bumped. A live file whose hash is `old` while the staged file hashes to `new` gets the staged file renamed over it, which bumps `version` once. A live file that matches neither was changed meanwhile: nothing is touched, the file, the staged content and the journal all stay, and the command fails with exit code 6 (`refused`) and a message that says `recovery required` and names the file and the journal, with a hint to restore the file's previous content and rerun, or to move the journal aside to keep the file as it is. A `.json` file under `mutations/` that is not a journal, or one whose staged file is missing or does not hash to `new`, is refused the same way. A step that cannot be applied at all, a directory that cannot be written or a path something else took, leaves the journal where it is and is exit code 6 (`refused`) with a message that says `recovery required` and names the step, its path and the journal: from the moment the journal exists every command recovers it before its own work, so a raw input or output message here would become every command's answer, with nothing in it to act on. A `ref` step whose repository git cannot read is exit code 8 (`account_repo`) instead, wherever it surfaces, since a journal reaches the account repo long after the command that wrote it is gone. A `publish` step whose staging directory is gone is decided from the live path: one still holding what the step expected lost its staged content, and one holding anything else changed after the publish renamed the content into place, which is what the refusal then names – the staging directory is supposed to be gone by then. A journal that replaces a directory records two steps at one path, a `remove` that takes the old content out of the way and a `publish` or a `link` that fills the path again: a path step whose path already holds what a later step of the same journal leaves there is done, since the path moved on past it, and a process stopped after both steps is finished rather than refused. A `move` is done the same way when a later `move` of the journal takes its path on and holds its own `new` state, so a directory a journal moves twice is never moved out of where it arrived. Recovery never repeats a completed step, so it can run any number of times. Journals are recovered oldest first and recovery stops at the first refusal. A `.staged` file without a journal, left by a process that stopped between the two writes, is removed once every journal is recovered.

A process that stops after the rename and before it rewrites `version`, like a recovery that finds the live file already new, leaves the new file in place without a `version` bump. That is a missed signal, not a lost change: `agentx serve` still rescans, since the file lives in agentx home, which it watches, and every other command reads the file fresh.

Every mutating command recovers before its own change, under the lock it already holds; a refused recovery refuses the command. Once the journals are recovered it removes what an interrupted command left of a pending merge, reading files alone unless there is something to remove (see **A merge pending** under [Skills](#skills)). `agentx scan` and every scan of `agentx serve` look for journals under the shared lock and, when one exists, release it, recover under the exclusive lock (waiting for a mutation in progress the way they wait for the shared lock), release that and start their reads over, so no inventory is composed from a half-applied change. A refused recovery is exit code 6 for `agentx scan` and for the initial scan of `agentx serve`; for a later scan of `agentx serve` it is a `log` warning on stderr and a `refresh_complete` with `ok: false` and the message in `error` for every pending refresh, while the last snapshot stays in force until the journal is resolved. `agentx doctor` reports unfinished journals in its `mutations` row and recovers nothing. Reading commands such as `config get` and `machine` neither check for journals nor recover.

## Content hash

The content hash identifies one version of a skill by its content. It is SHA-256 over the following byte sequence, where `NUL` is one zero byte:

1. the frontmatter `name`, then `NUL`;
2. the frontmatter `description`, then `NUL`;
3. for every regular file under the skill directory, in bytewise order of its relative path with `/` as the separator: the relative path, `NUL`, the byte length in decimal, `NUL`, the file bytes.

The frontmatter is the `---` block at the top of `SKILL.md`, read as YAML: `name` and `description` are taken from it in any scalar form the standard allows, a plain value, a quoted one, a literal or folded block, or a plain value continued on the indented lines below its key, with surrounding whitespace trimmed; a number or a boolean written without quotes contributes the text the file holds rather than the decoded value rendered back, so `007`, `1.50` and `True` hash as they are written and not as `7`, `1.5` and `true`; a key whose value is a mapping or a sequence contributes nothing, so a nested block such as `metadata` is skipped; and a block that is not valid YAML, an unclosed one and a duplicate key included, is unparsable. A `SKILL.md` comes from any repository a user adds, so a block longer than 65536 bytes, or one opening more than 256 lists or mappings, is refused unread: the parser builds its whole tree before it can report anything, and a block that nests far enough costs memory faster than bytes, which ends the process rather than raising an error. Published blocks run to a few hundred bytes. A missing name or description contributes empty bytes; a missing or unparsable frontmatter counts as both missing, even though the skill is then named after its directory. A symlink inside the skill is followed when it resolves inside the skill directory and skipped with a warning otherwise: a file symlink contributes the target's bytes under the symlink's path, a directory symlink is walked under the symlink's path, and a directory symlink that points back at a directory being walked is a loop, skipped with a warning. File modes and times do not contribute. The hash is rendered as bare lowercase hex wherever a field carries one.

Two hashes of a skill are worth telling apart – what is on this machine now, and the version it was recorded from – and each has one name at the top level of every event and every document that carries it. At the top level, `content_hash` is always the first: the content hash of a version found on this machine, which is the version a scan read from disk, or what a library directory holds at the moment the command read it. At the top level, `base_hash` is always the second: the content hash of a skill's [base version](#skills), the one its import commit records in its `Agentx-Content-Hash` trailer, whatever machine that commit was written on. Something that knows both carries both, under those two names; something that knows only the recorded version carries `base_hash` alone. No top-level field means one of them in one place and the other in another, so a consumer never has to know which command emitted a field to know which hash it holds. A third version, the newer upstream version an update check pinned as a skill's candidate, is named apart from both: `candidate_hash` in an `update_available` event, and inside the `candidate` object of a library skill, which describes that version and nothing else, `content_hash` beside that version's `upstream_commit`, the two fields a version is named by wherever it is described on its own.

## Snapshot

`agentx scan` inventories the machine and emits one `snapshot` event: every detected agent configuration, every skill each one can see, every MCP server each one declares and every plugin each one has installed, with the skills and servers a plugin provides, and every skill of the library with what agentx knows of it. It writes nothing and never starts or connects to a server unless `--handshake` is given. Two scans of an unchanged machine produce byte-identical events when `instance_id` is fixed.

`agentx scan --project <path>` adds the project-scope skills found under `<path>` in each detected configuration's project skills directories, read-only; `<path>` must exist, else exit code 5. `agentx scan --configuration <id>` names the configuration that changed; the id must be detected, else exit code 5 with a hint listing the detected ids, and the whole machine is scanned regardless, since a one-shot command has no earlier snapshot to reuse.

Without `--json`, the output starts with one summary line counting the machine, `<n> configurations, <n> skills, <n> servers, <n> plugins detected` (`1 skill`), counting skills, servers and plugins as the snapshot does, once each however many occurrences; then one section per configuration, each preceded by a blank line and headed `<name> (<id>)  <path>  enabled|disabled` and, when the configuration has any, `  <n> skills, <n> servers, <n> plugins` (`1 skill`, leaving out a count of zero, counting the skill and server occurrences of this configuration), followed by up to three labelled blocks, each present only when it has rows: `skills:` with one line per occurrence (skill name, kind, scope and placement path, followed by ` -> <resolved path>` for a symlink and by `  (plugin <name>)` for a placement inside a plugin; the name is sanitised and the two paths are quoted as [Streams](#streams) prescribes, a skill's frontmatter and the names of the directories it sits in being text agentx did not write); `servers:` with one line per server (name, transport, then the command line or the URL, both sanitised as [Streams](#streams) prescribes since a configuration file declares them, with `  (plugin <name>)` for a server a plugin provides, then `<n> tools` (`1 tool`) for a server with a signature, counting its tools, prompts, resources and resource templates together, ending with `(disabled)` for a server its client records as turned off); and `plugins:` with one `name  version` line per plugin, followed by what the plugin provides in this configuration as `<n> skills, <n> servers` (`1 skill`, leaving out a count of zero, nothing when both are zero), ending with `(disabled)` for a plugin its client records as disabled. The block labels are indented two spaces and their rows four; rows are sorted by name. A configuration with no rows at all says `no skills, servers or plugins`. With no configuration detected the output is `No agent configurations detected.` and a hint line instead. Environment and header values are never printed. Warnings go to stderr as `warning: <message>`.

The event:

| Field | Type | Meaning |
|---|---|---|
| `instance_id` | string | fresh per process, or `AGENTX_INSTANCE_ID` |
| `scan_counter` | integer | `1` for a one-shot scan |
| `machine` | object | `id`, the machine id, and `label` |
| `configurations` | array | the detected configurations, sorted by `id` |
| `skills` | array | the skill nodes, sorted by `physical_id` |
| `library` | array | one object per skill directory of the library, sorted by `name`: the [`library_skill`](#event-types) event `agentx skill list` emits for it, every field and nothing else, without `type` and `schema_version`, so its `kind` is `managed` or `unmanaged` and where a skill is published to and came from are its `source` and `upstream` fields; `[]` when the library holds no skill, or when git cannot read the account repo, which a warning then says (see below). A managed skill whose import branch the account repo holds but whose library directory is gone is not listed, and so has no `state` and no `drift`: it is named in `warnings` instead, one per skill, as `agentx skill list` names it (see **Listing** under [Skills](#skills)) |
| `mcp_servers` | array | the MCP server nodes, sorted by `physical_id` |
| `plugins` | array | the plugin nodes, sorted by `physical_id` |
| `edges` | array | `{"from", "to"}` pairs of node ids, sorted by `from` then `to`: the machine id to each configuration; each configuration to each skill it sees, each server it declares and each plugin it has installed; each plugin to each skill and server it provides |
| `warnings` | array of strings | what could not be read, sorted; a warning never fails the scan |

A configuration:

| Field | Type | Meaning |
|---|---|---|
| `id` | string | the configuration id: the client's slug, such as `claude-code`, `cursor`, `codex`, `gemini-cli`, `windsurf`, `github-copilot` |
| `client` | string | the client's slug |
| `name` | string | the client's display name |
| `path` | string | the user-scope configuration directory; the configuration is detected because it exists |
| `enabled` | boolean | `false` when the id is in `disabled_configurations` |
| `reads_library` | boolean | whether the client reads the library directly, so a library skill needs no placement in its own directory: a universal client, see [Skills](#skills). A client one of whose skills directories leads to the library directory through a symlink, or the library to it, reads the library too: the skill's directory there is the library entry itself |
| `physical_id`, `logical_id` | string | the same value, see below |

A skill node is one content version seen on this machine; the same content found through several placements is one node with several occurrences:

| Field | Type | Meaning |
|---|---|---|
| `physical_id`, `logical_id` | string | see below |
| `name` | string | the frontmatter `name`, or the directory name when the frontmatter is missing, unparsable or has no name |
| `description` | string | the frontmatter `description`, or empty |
| `content_hash` | string | the content hash, lowercase hex |
| `occurrences` | array | sorted by `id` |

An occurrence:

| Field | Type | Meaning |
|---|---|---|
| `id` | string | see below |
| `configuration` | string | the configuration id |
| `path` | string | the placement path as the client sees it |
| `resolved_path` | string | the real directory after following every symlink |
| `kind` | string | `symlink` when the placement path is a symlink; `copy` when it is a real directory in user scope and `copy_mode` lists the configuration under the directory's name; `directory` for every other real directory |
| `scope` | string | `user`, or `project` under `--project` |
| `plugin` | string | the name of the plugin the placement is inside; absent for every other placement |

An MCP server node is one server in one signature version; the same server declared in several configurations is one node with several occurrences:

| Field | Type | Meaning |
|---|---|---|
| `physical_id`, `logical_id` | string | see below |
| `name` | string | the key the server is declared under, from its first declaration in scan order |
| `signature` | string | the signature hash, see the handshake below: what the server exposed to this scan's handshake, else the last one stored in `handshakes.json`; `none`, the fixed no-signature marker, when neither exists |
| `signature_at` | string | when the signature was taken, RFC 3339 in UTC; absent with `none` |
| `tools` | array | what the server exposed, one `{"id", "kind", "name", "description"}` per tool, prompt, resource or resource template, `kind` being `tool`, `prompt`, `resource` or `resource_template`, sorted by `kind` then `name`; absent with `none` or when the server exposed nothing. No edge points at a tool: it is reached through its server |
| `occurrences` | array | sorted by `id` |

A server occurrence:

| Field | Type | Meaning |
|---|---|---|
| `id` | string | see below |
| `configuration` | string | the configuration id |
| `config_file` | string | the file the server is declared in |
| `command` | string | the command of a local server, or empty |
| `args` | array of strings | its arguments, in order |
| `env_keys` | array of strings | the names of the environment variables the declaration sets, sorted; the values are never recorded |
| `url` | string | the URL of a remote server as written, or empty |
| `header_keys` | array of strings | the names of the headers the declaration sets, sorted; the values are never recorded |
| `transport` | string | `stdio` when the declaration has a command; `sse` when its `type` or `transport` says so, or, for Gemini CLI, when it uses `url` rather than `httpUrl`; `streamable-http` for every other URL |
| `handshake` | boolean | whether this scan handshook the declaration: `true` only under `--handshake` and only when the handshake succeeded |
| `handshake_error` | object | `{"reason", "message", "hint"}` when this scan's handshake of the declaration failed, see the handshake below; absent from every other occurrence |
| `plugin` | string | the name of the plugin that declares the server; absent for a configuration's own servers |
| `enabled` | boolean | `false` when the client records this declaration as turned off: a Codex declaration with `enabled = false`, a Codex plugin server turned off by its overlay, every server of a Codex plugin with `enabled = false` (see below), a Gemini CLI server listed in `mcp.excluded` of `~/.gemini/settings.json`, left out of a non-empty `mcp.allowed` there, or recorded as `{"enabled": false}` under its lowercased name in `~/.gemini/mcp-server-enablement.json`, and a Copilot CLI server listed in `disabledMcpServers` of `~/.copilot/settings.json`. Claude Code, Cursor and Windsurf keep that state per project or inside the app, and it is not read; absent from every other occurrence, including one the client records as on. A turned-off declaration is still declared and still merges into its server node by identity, and is never handshaken |

A plugin node is one plugin bundle installed in one configuration, in one version:

| Field | Type | Meaning |
|---|---|---|
| `physical_id`, `logical_id` | string | see below |
| `name` | string | the plugin name |
| `marketplace` | string | where the plugin was installed from: the marketplace name for Claude Code and Codex, the marketplace directory name for a cached Cursor plugin (`cursor-public` is the official marketplace), the recorded install source for a Gemini CLI extension; empty for a Cursor plugin under `plugins/local` and whenever the client records none |
| `version` | string | the installed version, or empty when the client records none |
| `configuration` | string | the configuration id |
| `path` | string | the plugin's directory |
| `enabled` | boolean | whether the client has the plugin enabled; present only when the client records that state on disk, which today is Codex for a plugin with a `config.toml` table; absent for every other plugin, which is installed but not known to be enabled or disabled |

Server discovery reads these user-scope files, each parsed as a map of server name to declaration under its top-level key, ignoring unknown keys; a declaration that is not an object or has neither a command nor a URL is ignored; a missing file declares nothing; a file that cannot be read or parsed is one warning naming the file, never its content, and the scan continues:

| Configuration | File | Format |
|---|---|---|
| `claude-code` | `~/.claude.json` | JSON, `mcpServers` |
| `codex` | `~/.codex/config.toml` | TOML, `[mcp_servers.<name>]` tables with `command`, `args`, `env`, `cwd`, `url`, `http_headers`, `env_http_headers`, `bearer_token_env_var` and `enabled` |
| `cursor` | `~/.cursor/mcp.json` | JSON, `mcpServers` |
| `gemini-cli` | `~/.gemini/settings.json` | JSON, `mcpServers`, with `url` an SSE endpoint and `httpUrl` a streamable HTTP one; `mcp.excluded` and `mcp.allowed` turn servers off |
| `windsurf` | `~/.codeium/windsurf/mcp_config.json` | JSON, `mcpServers`, with `url` or `serverUrl` |
| `github-copilot` | `~/.copilot/mcp-config.json` | JSON, `mcpServers`; `disabledMcpServers` in `~/.copilot/settings.json` turns servers off |

In the JSON files a declaration holds `command`, `args`, `env` and an optional `cwd` for a local server, `url` (or the client's variant) and `headers` for a remote one, and an optional `type` or `transport`. Every other configuration contributes skills only.

The library is read with the rest of the scan, under the same shared lock, so that no entry pairs a library directory with lineage from either side of a mutation: its directories as a client that reads the library reads them, through the same reads, so a skill is hashed once however many ways the scan reaches it and what cannot be read in the library is one of the snapshot's warnings; the lineage branches in one `for-each-ref` over `refs/heads/managed/` and `refs/heads/skills/`, after the `rev-parse` that checks the account repo; and the sources of the settings, which decide `drift`. A machine without an account repo spawns no git for it and lists every library skill as unmanaged. An account repo git cannot read does not fail the scan, since nothing else in the snapshot is read from it: `library` is `[]`, rather than every skill listed as unmanaged, which would not be true, and one warning, `<what skill list fails with>; the library is not listed, run 'agentx doctor' and check the account repo it names`, names the account repo and git's error, while the rest of the snapshot is what it would have been; a later scan that can read the repo again lists the library, and under [serve](#serve) that is a changed snapshot. The warning sends the reader to the account repo as well as to doctor because git's error is the diagnosis: doctor checks that the repo opens, and a repo that opens can still hold a branch git cannot read, one whose commit is missing for instance. `skill list`, which reports nothing but the library, is exit code 8 on the same repo. A skill of the library is also a skill node wherever a client sees it: the node is the content a client sees, the entry what agentx knows of the directory. Without `--json` the library is not printed, [`agentx skill list`](#skills) printing it, and its lineage is not read: a text scan, and every scan of a text serve, spawn no git for the library's lineage and cannot fail on the account repo, though a long-running serve still reads the source refs for its source index after each scan, as [Serve](#serve) describes. Its directories are read all the same, so a scan warns about the same library directories in both modes.

### Handshake

`agentx scan --handshake` starts or connects to every declared server after the local reads, outside the lock, at most four at a time, and lists what each exposes. Each server has a budget of ten seconds; `AGENTX_HANDSHAKE_TIMEOUT`, a duration such as `500ms`, replaces it for tests and diagnosis. A declaration its client has turned off (`enabled: false` on its occurrence) is never started or connected to, and keeps its stored signature. A server on the `stdio` transport is started as its command and arguments in its declared `cwd`, a relative one resolved against the plugin's directory for a plugin's server and otherwise against the directory `agentx` runs in, as each client resolves it against its own; without one, it starts in the directory `agentx` runs in, or, for a server of a Codex plugin in the Agent Plugins format, in the plugin's directory; a command with a `/` resolves against that working directory, a bare one is looked up on the `PATH` of the `agentx` process. It runs with the environment of the `agentx` process plus the variables the declaration sets, and its standard error goes to the null device; when the handshake is over its standard input is closed, a server still running a second later receives `SIGTERM`, and one still running a second after that is killed. A remote server receives the declared headers; for Codex, an `env_http_headers` value is read from the environment of the `agentx` process, and a `bearer_token_env_var` that environment sets becomes `Authorization: Bearer <value>` (listed in `header_keys`; unset, no `Authorization` is sent). A server on `streamable-http` receives JSON-RPC by `POST` to its URL, `Accept: application/json, text/event-stream`, and answers either as JSON or as an event stream; the `Mcp-Session-Id` the server assigns is echoed and released with `DELETE` at the end. A remote server is reached through the proxy the environment of the `agentx` process names (`HTTPS_PROXY`, `HTTP_PROXY`, `NO_PROXY`), the one place a handshake reads that environment directly. The exchange is `initialize` with protocol version `2025-11-25`, the last MCP revision that opens with an `initialize` handshake (the server's answer is accepted whatever version it names; a server that implements only the `2026-07-28` revision or later, which carries the version on every request instead, rejects `initialize` and is one warning), `notifications/initialized`, then `tools/list`, `prompts/list`, `resources/list` and `resources/templates/list`, each only when the initialize result declares its capability, following `nextCursor` until the last page. A method the server reports as unknown (JSON-RPC error `-32601`) contributes nothing.

Before a declaration is handshaken, the placeholders in its command, arguments, environment values, `cwd`, URL and header values are expanded from the environment of the `agentx` process the way its client expands them. The expansion serves the handshake alone: the snapshot records every value as written, so a server's identity never depends on the environment, and no expanded value is printed or stored. A default applies only when the variable is unset; a variable set to the empty string gives the empty string.

| Configuration | Placeholders | An unset variable |
|---|---|---|
| `claude-code` | `${VAR}`, `${VAR:-default}` | stays as written. In a remote server's URL and headers, the variables Claude Code treats as credentials are empty whether set or not, a default notwithstanding: the list of Claude Code 2.1.278 (such as `AWS_SECRET_ACCESS_KEY`, `NPM_TOKEN`, `HTTPS_PROXY` and any `OTEL_*`), matched without regard to case and with an `INPUT_` prefix, any `*_BASE_URL` whose value embeds credentials, and, beyond that list, every `ANTHROPIC_*` and `CLAUDE_*` variable, so that one a later Claude Code protects stays withheld while the list lags. With `--verbose`, a debug line names the variables withheld from each server, never their values |
| `cursor` | `${env:NAME}`, `${userHome}`, `${pathSeparator}`, `${/}` | stays as written, and so do `${workspaceFolder}` and `${workspaceFolderBasename}`, a user-scope declaration having no workspace |
| `gemini-cli` | `$VAR`, `${VAR}`, `${VAR:-default}` | stays as written, except in an environment value or a header, where it is empty |
| `windsurf` | `${env:NAME}`, and `${file:/path}` for the file's contents, trimmed | is empty; a file that cannot be read stays as written |
| `github-copilot` | `$VAR`, `${VAR}`, `${VAR:-default}`, and `~` at the start of `cwd` for the home directory | stays as written |
| `codex` | none; the environment enters through `env_http_headers` and `bearer_token_env_var` | |

A server on `sse` speaks the legacy HTTP+SSE transport of protocol revision `2024-11-05`. A `GET` to its URL with the declared headers and `Accept: text/event-stream` opens an event stream whose first `endpoint` event names, relative to the URL, where each JSON-RPC message is `POST`ed with the same headers; the answers arrive on the stream as `message` events. An endpoint on another scheme or host than the URL is refused before anything is sent to it, since the declared headers would follow. The stream is closed at the end. A `streamable-http` server whose opening `POST` is refused with HTTP 400, 404 or 405 and a body that is not a JSON-RPC error (which is how a current server refuses a method) is tried once more on this transport at the same URL, as the protocol's backwards compatibility asks; when that URL opens no event stream, the original refusal is the failure. No other URL is ever tried: a handshake reaches the server where its client does.

A server that cannot be started, exits, answers something that is not JSON-RPC, refuses a method with any other error or runs out of its budget is one warning, `<config file>: server <name>: <reason>`, that never carries an environment or header value; the scan continues and the server keeps its stored signature, or `none`. The exit code stays 0.

The occurrence of a failed declaration carries `handshake_error`: `message` is the reason of the warning, `hint` is what the user can do about it, and `reason` is one of:

| `reason` | When |
|---|---|
| `unauthorized` | the server answered HTTP 401 or 403. The handshake sends only the declared headers and never the sign-in an agent client holds, so a server behind OAuth always fails this way; the hint says so, or, for a Codex `bearer_token_env_var`, to set or renew that variable, or, when the declaration sets headers, that they were refused |
| `unreachable` | the connection failed, or the server answered HTTP 404 or 410 without a JSON-RPC error |
| `not_found` | the command of a local server does not exist |
| `timeout` | the budget ran out |
| `failed` | everything else: the server exited, answered something that is not JSON-RPC, another HTTP status or a JSON-RPC error, or its event stream named an endpoint on another origin |

In human output the server's row ends with the failure in words (`needs sign-in`, `unreachable`, `command not found`, `timed out`, `handshake failed`), after the tool count of a stored signature, and stderr closes with one `hint:` line per distinct hint, after the warnings.

Every item is serialised as its kind, name, description, input schema and output schema joined by `NUL`, the schemas as canonical JSON (object keys sorted, no whitespace, numbers as written, only the escapes JSON requires) and empty for anything but a tool or an absent schema. Items are sorted by kind, then name, then serialisation. The signature is SHA-256 over the serialisations joined by `NUL`, rendered as 64 lowercase hex characters; a server that exposes nothing has the signature of the empty list. A tool's hash is SHA-256 over its own serialisation, and its `id` is the identity `tool` over the server's logical id and that hash, so a tool that is byte-identical in two versions of one server is one node.

After the handshakes, the signatures taken are written to `handshakes.json` in agentx home under the exclusive lock, replacing the entry of each server handshaken and keeping every other: `{"<server logical id>": {"signature": "<hex>", "tools": [...], "at": "<RFC 3339>"}}`, `tools` as on the node. The file is derived state, so `version` is not rewritten: no other consumer needs to rescan for it. When one scan handshakes two declarations of one logical server that answer differently, each declaration gets its own node, and the file keeps the declaration last in scan order. A scan without `--handshake` reads the file and gives every declaration of a listed server the stored `signature`, `signature_at` and `tools`; a file that cannot be parsed is a warning and counts as empty.

Plugin discovery reads, per configuration:

| Configuration | Install records | Plugin directories | Manifest |
|---|---|---|---|
| `claude-code` | `~/.claude/plugins/installed_plugins.json`, keyed `<name>@<marketplace>`, each record with `installPath` and `version` | the `installPath` | `.claude-plugin/plugin.json`, for the version when the record has none |
| `codex` | the `[plugins."<name>@<marketplace>"]` tables of `~/.codex/config.toml`, each with `enabled` (`true` when absent) | `~/.codex/plugins/cache/<marketplace>/<name>/<version>/`; the active version is `local` when present, else the highest by semver, compared as strings where either name is not a semver | `plugin.json` at the root when its `$schema` starts with `https://agent-plugins.org/schemas/`, else the first of `.codex-plugin/plugin.json`, `.claude-plugin/plugin.json` and `.cursor-plugin/plugin.json` |
| `cursor` | none on disk: the account holds them | `~/.cursor/plugins/local/<dir>` and `~/.cursor/plugins/cache/<marketplace>/<name>/<version>/` holding a `.cache-complete` file | the first of `.cursor-plugin/plugin.json`, `.claude-plugin/plugin.json` and `plugin.json` |
| `gemini-cli` | none: every directory under `~/.gemini/extensions` holding a `gemini-extension.json` is an extension | that directory | `gemini-extension.json`, which also declares the `mcpServers`, with `${extensionPath}` replaced by the extension directory and `${/}` and `${pathSeparator}` by the path separator in the command, the arguments, the environment values and `cwd`; the marketplace is the `source` in `.gemini-extension-install.json` next to it, when present |

Every other configuration reports no plugins. For every client, a manifest or server file that cannot be parsed is one warning naming the file, never its content, and the plugin is still reported with what its directory gives; a missing file is silent. The skills a plugin provides are discovered in its skills directories exactly like a skills directory (direct children only), merged with every other skill by content hash and recorded as occurrences carrying the plugin's name; its servers are recorded as occurrences carrying the plugin's name with the file they were read from as `config_file`.

Claude Code: a record without an `installPath` or whose directory does not exist is a warning; skills are under `skills` and servers in `.mcp.json` at the root, in the `mcpServers` JSON format, with `${CLAUDE_PLUGIN_ROOT}` replaced by the plugin directory in the command, the arguments, the environment values and `cwd`.

Codex: the name and marketplace are the two halves of the `config.toml` key, split on its last `@`; a key without both halves is a warning, and a key whose plugin directory does not exist is a warning naming that directory. A plugin directory without a key, which is how a plugin installed through the account appears, is reported without an `enabled` field. The version is the manifest's `version`, else the version directory name. Skills are the manifest's `skills` paths (one string or a list, each starting with `./` and staying inside the plugin, else a warning naming the path), else `skills`. Servers are the manifest's `mcpServers` object, recorded with the manifest as `config_file`, or the file that field names, else `.mcp.json`, else `mcp.json`; the file holds the Codex `config.toml` fields (`command`, `args`, `env`, `cwd`, `url`, `http_headers`, `env_http_headers`, `bearer_token_env_var`, `enabled`) as JSON, either under `mcpServers` or as a bare map of name to declaration. A `[plugins."<name>@<marketplace>".mcp_servers.<server>]` table with `enabled = false` marks that server's occurrence `enabled: false`; its other keys are ignored. A plugin with `enabled = false` marks every server it provides the same way. In a plugin in the Agent Plugins format, `${PLUGIN_ROOT}` is replaced by the plugin directory in the command, the arguments, the environment values and `cwd`.

Cursor: a plugin under `plugins/local` has an empty `marketplace`; a hidden entry or a file there is skipped, and a symlink is followed only when it resolves inside `plugins/local`, else it is a warning naming the link. A cached plugin takes its `marketplace` and `name` from the directory names, the manifest's `name` replacing the latter when present, and its `version` from the manifest, else the version directory name (a commit id or `release_<tag>`); an entry without `.cache-complete` is a warning naming the directory and reports no plugin, and every complete version is reported. A plugin without a manifest is named after its directory. Cursor records neither what is installed nor what is enabled on disk, so a reported plugin is installed as far as the cache shows, not necessarily enabled, and its node has no `enabled` field. Skills are in the manifest's `skills` paths (one or a list, each relative, with or without a leading `./` as Cursor's own marketplace plugins omit it, and staying inside the plugin, else a warning naming the path; a path holding a `SKILL.md` is that one skill, another is a skills directory), else under `skills`; servers are the manifest's `mcpServers` object, with the manifest as the `config_file`, else the file the manifest names, else `mcp.json`, else `.mcp.json`, in the `mcpServers` JSON format, with `${CURSOR_PLUGIN_ROOT}` and `${CLAUDE_PLUGIN_ROOT}` replaced by the plugin directory in the command, the arguments, the environment values and `cwd`. The Claude Code plugins Cursor imports from `~/.claude/plugins` are reported under `claude-code` only, never a second time under `cursor`.

Discovery: inside each skills directory a client reads, every child directory, or symlink to one, that holds a `SKILL.md` is a skill; hidden entries and `node_modules` are skipped; a broken symlink is a warning naming the link, and several in one directory are one warning naming the directory, their count and their names. A client's user-scope skills directories are its own, other clients' directories it reads (Cursor reads the Claude Code and Codex directories) and the library for clients that read it directly (Codex and Gemini CLI), which yields an occurrence with the library path as placement path whatever the enabled state. Each directory is canonicalised once and each skill is hashed once per scan.

Identities are SHA-256 over the type name and its inputs, each input preceded by one `NUL` byte, rendered as `<type>:<lowercase hex>`. Paths under the user's home are hashed with the home replaced by `~`, so an identity does not depend on where the home directory is. Every identity but a skill's, a plugin's and a remote or package server's logical one includes the machine id; since the platform-derived id also covers the user id, a snapshot that must compare across users or machines, such as the CLI's own golden files, pins the id with `machine.json`.

| Identity | Inputs |
|---|---|
| machine node id | the machine id itself, no hash |
| configuration | `configuration`, machine id, configuration id, configuration path |
| skill, logical | `skill`, `content`, content hash: an unmanaged skill is its content |
| skill, physical | `skill`, logical id, machine id, content hash |
| skill occurrence | `occurrence`, configuration physical id, skill physical id, scope, placement path |
| server, logical, remote | `server`, `url`, the normalised URL: lowercase scheme and host, no user information, no default port (80 for `http`, 443 for `https`), no trailing slash, no query or fragment; a remote server is one whose URL has a host other than `localhost` or an IP address |
| server, logical, package | `server`, the registry, the package: `npm` and the first non-flag argument of `npx` without its `@version`; `pypi` and the first non-flag argument of `uvx` or of `pipx run` before any version specifier or extras; `docker` and the first argument of `docker run` that is neither a flag nor the value of one (`-e`, `-v`, `-p`, `--name`, `-w`, `--network`, `--entrypoint`, `-u`, `--mount`, `--env-file`, `--platform`, `-l`, `--add-host`, `-h` and their long forms), without its tag or digest |
| server, logical, other | `server`, `machine`, machine id, command, URL, each argument, with the home replaced by `~` in the command and the arguments: a server that is neither remote nor a package never matches one on another machine |
| server, physical | `server`, logical id, machine id, signature |
| tool | `tool`, server logical id, tool hash |
| server occurrence | `occurrence`, configuration physical id, server physical id, config file, server name |
| plugin, logical | `plugin`, marketplace, name |
| plugin, physical | `plugin`, logical id, machine id, configuration physical id, version |

## Git

The CLI runs the system git as a subprocess and never embeds a git implementation. Git is located through `PATH` of the environment the CLI was given. The floor is git 2.40, compared by numeric version components, so 2.4 and 2.39 are rejected and 2.100 is accepted. Before any command other than `version`, `doctor` and `help` runs, the CLI runs `git --version` once; a missing or too-old git is exit code 2 with a hint naming the distribution package where known, before the command does anything. `version` and `doctor` stay available to report the problem.

Every git call names its repository with `--git-dir` explicitly, but for the calls run in a pending merge's worktree, which name it by their working directory (see below), captures stdout and stderr, and logs the command line and stderr at debug level. A command that replaces the directory the process works in, an update of a skill run in the skill's directory, enters it again by its path once it is replaced, so no git it runs afterwards inherits a working directory that is gone. Git runs in one of two environments, built from the CLI's own environment:

- Isolated, for every command that writes objects or merges, and for every other local read of the account repo: `GIT_CONFIG_GLOBAL` points at `/dev/null`, `GIT_CONFIG_NOSYSTEM=1`, every `GIT_*` variable of the user's is dropped, author and committer are fixed to `agentx <agentx@localhost>` at `946684800 +0000`, `GIT_NO_LAZY_FETCH=1` forbids fetching a missing object one at a time (git 2.45 and newer honour it; older gits ignore it), `GIT_ATTR_NOSYSTEM=1`, and `core.autocrlf=false`, `commit.gpgsign=false`, `core.hooksPath=/dev/null` and `core.attributesFile=/dev/null` are passed with `-c`, so no attributes file of the user's is read either, not even the one git reads under `$XDG_CONFIG_HOME/git/` when no configuration names one, and no merge driver, filter or marker size of the user's or the system's changes what git writes. A commit made this way has the same id on every machine whatever the user's git configuration, and nothing in this environment touches the network.
- User, for network commands: the fetches of `source add`, `source fetch`, `skill add` and `skill check-updates`, and of the update check of the [serve](#serve) child, which fetches every source; the access check of a source and the read of its default branch, run unattended (see **Unattended git** below); the `ls-remote`, fetch and push of the [account remote](#account-remote), which also pass `-c core.hooksPath=/dev/null`, and `--no-verify` to the push, so that no hook of the user's global configuration runs in the account repo; and the one read of the user's `core.excludesFile` described below. The CLI's environment without the variables that tell git which repository to use or where to look for one (`GIT_DIR`, `GIT_WORK_TREE`, `GIT_INDEX_FILE`, `GIT_OBJECT_DIRECTORY`, `GIT_ALTERNATE_OBJECT_DIRECTORIES`, `GIT_COMMON_DIR`, `GIT_NAMESPACE`, `GIT_CEILING_DIRECTORIES`, `GIT_DISCOVERY_ACROSS_FILESYSTEM`, `GIT_PREFIX`), so credential helpers, SSH configuration, proxy settings and URL rewrites (`url.<base>.insteadOf`) apply, while a repository a shell or a git hook names, such as an exported `GIT_DIR`, is never read: `ls-remote`, which names no repository, would otherwise take its URL rewrites and remotes and reach another URL than the fetch that follows. A variable agentx sets for one call, such as the `GIT_INDEX_FILE` of a throwaway index, applies on top of either environment. agentx never stores git credentials.

A git run that names no working directory of its own runs in the process's, unless that directory is gone, as it is for a shell left in a pending merge's checkout once an update completes the merge and removes it: git then runs at the file system's root, so a command run from there still reads the account repo it names with `--git-dir`. An isolated call may name its own author and committer dates, which is how the [import commit](#skills) takes the upstream committer's time; everything else the isolated environment fixes still holds. A command that has several read-only calls to make that do not depend on each other runs them at once through the git runner, at most four at a time, the bound the fetches of a source and the handshakes of a scan keep: the calls come back in the order they were given, the first failure by that order is the error, and the number of processes is the same as running them one after another. Only reads run this way; every write is made by one call at a time. The refs a reader takes as lineage, the branches, the candidates and the upstream-removed markers, are written under the lock and through a [mutation journal](#mutation-journal). What those refs will hold is written before, outside the lock, so that a slow network never holds it: the objects of a fetch and of an import, the staging refs that keep them until the journal points a branch or a candidate at them, and the source ref a whole fetch publishes, whose write is one `update-ref` that moves a ref nothing takes as lineage. A skill directory is compared as a work tree of the account repo: git runs in the isolated environment with `GIT_INDEX_FILE` naming a throwaway index in the temporary directory, `--work-tree` naming the skill directory and that directory as its working directory, `-c core.safecrlf=false`, which keeps line-ending warnings off stderr, and `-c core.excludesFile=<path>` when the user's own configuration names a global ignore file, which the isolated environment would otherwise lose. That path is read once per run, and once per serve child, the first time git runs over a skill directory, with `git config --path --get core.excludesFile` in the user environment; a key that is not set, or a configuration git cannot read, is no file, and git's default place for it, `$XDG_CONFIG_HOME/git/ignore`, needs no setting. Before each such run the account repo's `info/exclude` is brought in line with `ignore_system_files`. The `add -A` of such a run writes into the account repo the blobs and trees of the directory, which no ref holds, and a reader, `skill list` and serve among them, does so without taking the exclusive lock: git writes an object whole or not at all, an object written twice is the same object, and nothing agentx runs drops an object no ref holds: `gc.auto` is off, and the serve child's maintenance (see [Serve](#serve)) packs objects and refs and deletes none. `agentx skill update` writes the objects of a merge the same way, before the lock: the commit that holds the tree git wrote of an edited skill's library directory and the tree `merge-tree` writes, which no ref holds. The worktree of a pending merge is a linked worktree of the account repo, added with `worktree add --detach --lock --reason "agentx pending merge"`, after `worktree prune` when `worktree list --porcelain` shows a registration git calls prunable, and removed with `worktree remove -f -f`, which removes one that is locked. A fork's worktree is added with `worktree add --no-checkout --lock --reason "agentx fork" <path> skills/<name>`, after `worktree prune` when `worktree list --porcelain` shows a registration whose directory is gone; prune never takes a locked worktree, a fork's or a pending merge's, whatever became of its directory. Before it adds a fork's worktree, agentx clears with files alone what an earlier one left of that path, which `worktree remove` and a plain `worktree add` refuse: a lone `.git` file, a registration whose `gitdir` names the path, as a worktree removed by hand or an add killed part way leaves, and an admin directory with no `gitdir` that holds nothing but the fork's lock, which git skips when it lists or prunes worktrees. Serve prunes the same way when it starts, and `worktree repair`, which serve runs when it starts and `skill place` runs for a fork, both for a worktree whose pointers no longer meet, as moving agentx home leaves them, is only ever run with the paths of the worktrees it repairs named, and with `--relative-paths` when the account repo writes relative worktree paths (see [Account repo](#account-repo)). Git runs in the worktree in the isolated environment with the worktree as its working directory and no `--git-dir`, and `-c core.fileMode=true -c core.symlinks=true` (see **Conflicts** under [Skills](#skills)).

**Fork commits.** Every commit agentx writes on a fork's branch, the commit `skill new` or `skill fork` writes to create one of your own skills and every later one, is written by one writer: `commit-tree` in the isolated environment, so no hook runs, nothing signs it and no setting or attribute of the user's changes it, with author and committer passed explicitly as the user's own identity and dated now in the local time zone, or the one `TZ` names. The identity and the user's `core.excludesFile` are read once per command, in one `git config -z --get-regexp '^(user\.(name|email)|core\.excludesfile)$'` in the user environment against the account repo, so every configuration file git reads applies, included files and all, and the last value of a key wins; an excludes file that needs expanding, one starting with `~` or naming `%(prefix)`, is read again with `config --path --get core.excludesFile`, the read every other command makes of it. A user with no `user.name` or `user.email`, or one with nothing left once `<`, `>` and line breaks are taken out, is written as the machine: its label as the name and `machine-<the first 12 characters of the machine id>@agentx.invalid` as the address. The tree of a commit with one parent is what git writes of the fork's skill directory as a work tree over a throwaway index, as a skill directory is compared above, so Git's ignore rules apply: the skill's own `.gitignore` files, a `.gitignore` at the root of the fork's worktree, the user's global ignore file, at `core.excludesFile` or git's default place, and the account repo's `info/exclude`, which holds the `ignore_system_files` list while the setting is on, is empty while it is off, and is brought in line before every fork command. A file git ignores stays on disk and is never committed. Attributes come from the skill's own `.gitattributes` alone, `core.attributesFile` pointing at the null device. A Git repository nested in the skill directory that the ignore rules do not cover is refused with exit code 6, naming its path, rather than committed as a link to a commit of its own; one they cover is left where it is. The commit carries the trailers of [Forks](#skills): `Agentx-Machine` always, `Agentx-Fork-ID` on a creation commit but a rename's, and `Agentx-Base` on a merge. agentx writes a commit only when a command is asked to, never on a timer, and never amends one; the root tree of a commit with one parent on an existing branch is the tip's with the skill's directory entry replaced, so an entry beside it that a commit made with git put there stays. A merge commit has two parents and the merge's tree: what `merge-tree` wrote for a clean merge, or, for one completed from a pending merge, the index of its resolved worktree (see **A fork's merge pending** under [Skills](#skills)). A message given with `-m` that ends in trailers of its own, such as `Signed-off-by`, gets agentx's trailers added to that block, so that git still reads the user's as trailers.

**Commits on a shared source.** The commit a publish pushes to a shared source (see **Publishing a skill of a shared source** under [Skills](#skills)) is written with `commit-tree` in the isolated environment, so no hook runs and nothing signs it, with the user's own `user.name` and `user.email`, read as **Fork commits** reads them, as its author and committer, dated now; a user with neither is refused rather than written as the machine. It has one parent, the commit the source's branch was fetched at, and its tree is that commit's with the skill's edits applied to the skill's folder alone: one `diff-tree -r -z --no-renames` of the base version against the tree git writes of the library directory gives the change set, which `update-index -z --index-info` applies, under the folder's path, to a throwaway index loaded with `read-tree <fetched commit>`, and `write-tree --missing-ok` writes the tree. So every other entry of the branch, a symlink or a file agentx does not install included, stays as the branch holds it, and none of the blobs a blobless fetch left on the remote is needed: every call runs with `GIT_NO_LAZY_FETCH=1`. A commit whose `diff-tree` against its parent names a path outside the folder is exit code 10, and nothing is pushed. Its message is `-m` as given, or `Update <folder>`, the folder's path in the repository, or, for a skill at the root of its repository, `Update <name of its directory>`, with no trailer: no `Agentx-*` line, fork id or machine id reaches a shared source, and no fork branch is ever pushed to one. The push is unsigned, sends no thin pack, fetches nothing lazily and is leased on the fetched commit, in the user environment, so the user's URL rewrites, credentials and an `includeIf` of theirs apply to it as they do to a fetch of the source.

**Unattended git.** A check the user did not ask for, the access check of a source and the read of its default branch (see **Access** under [Sources](#sources)), runs unattended, as every git call of the serve child does, within 30 seconds. Users key settings on a remote's URL with `includeIf "hasconfig:remote.*.url:..."`, and inside the account repo every source's URL matches at once, so that an SSH command, a credential or any other setting included for one source would apply to all of them. So both run with `--git-dir` naming an empty throwaway repository in the temporary directory, a `HEAD`, `objects` and `refs` and no config file, and the source's remote given on the command line: `-c remote.agentx.url=<url>`. Only the user's settings keyed on the source's own URL apply there, and the ones keyed on no URL; the URL is the source's canonical URL, as `source list` prints it, so a pattern keyed on it must match that form, `ssh://git@github-work/**` and not the shorthand `git@github-work:*/**`; a setting keyed on agentx home's directory with `includeIf "gitdir:..."` does not, though it applies to the source's fetches, which run in the account repo; `GIT_SSH_COMMAND`, when the user sets it, applies to every source, as it does in git. The check passes `--no-signed`, so a user's `push.gpgSign` never asks for a push certificate.

In the serve child every git call runs unattended, so a prompt becomes an error rather than a hang: in a session of its own, so that neither git nor the ssh it starts has a controlling terminal to ask on, with stdin the null device and `GIT_TERMINAL_PROMPT=0`, `GIT_ASKPASS=/bin/false`, `SSH_ASKPASS_REQUIRE=never`, `SSH_ASKPASS=/bin/false`, for an ssh older than OpenSSH 8.4 that knows no `SSH_ASKPASS_REQUIRE`, and `GCM_INTERACTIVE=never`. A credential helper that answers without asking and a running SSH agent still answer. The user's SSH command, `GIT_SSH_COMMAND` or `core.sshCommand`, is left as it is: ssh with no terminal cannot ask for a passphrase or to trust a host key, and fails instead.

## Doctor

`agentx doctor` checks whether this machine can run agentx and reports one row per check, in this order. It only reads: it creates neither agentx home, nor the lock file, nor the account repo, and its git probes run in a throwaway repository in the temporary directory.

| Check | Statuses | What it means |
|---|---|---|
| `git` | `ok`, `fail` | git is in `PATH` and 2.40 or newer; the detail names the version |
| `fork_merges` | `ok`, `fail` | upstream changes can be merged into forks: `git merge-tree --write-tree --merge-base=<base> <ours> <theirs>` merges two branches of a throwaway repository in the temporary directory, made with deterministic commits |
| `commit_identity` | `ok`, `fail` | commits get the same id on every machine: the isolated environment gives a fixed input the known commit id `5d75017e77f5413f4337ef776244b8d8dc77ca90`; the failure detail names both ids |
| `home` | `ok`, `fail` | agentx home is writable, or does not exist yet; the first scan creates it |
| `lock` | `ok`, `warn` | the lock is free, a missing lock file included, or held by another agentx command; the detail names the holder's process id |
| `mutations` | `ok`, `warn`, `fail` | no [mutation journal](#mutation-journal) is unfinished; a warning names the count and the oldest journal, and the hint says how to recover; doctor recovers nothing itself; `fail` when the directory cannot be read |
| `settings` | `ok`, `fail` | `settings.json` parses, or does not exist yet; the detail and hint name the path. A file whose `schema_version` is later than this agentx reads fails too, though it parses: the detail names the path and both versions, and the hint is `upgrade agentx to the version that wrote it`, since fixing or deleting the file would lose what the later version keeps in it |
| `account_repo` | `ok`, `fail` | the account repo opens, or does not exist yet |
| `source_remotes` | `ok`, `warn`, `fail` | every source remote of the account repo is a source the settings name (see [Sources](#sources)); a warning names the count and each remote by the canonical URL it records, or by its source id for a URL agentx would not have written, and the hint gives the same two repairs a failed take-back gives, `agentx source add <url>` or `agentx source remove <id>`, so that the two report one thing once, except that a remote whose one fetch refspec is that of fork branches is never added back as a shared source, which would fetch its forks without blobs: while the settings hold no account remote it is one they lost, to be added back with `agentx source add <url> --account` (of several, one is chosen and the others removed), and while they hold one it is what replacing that one left, to be cleared with `agentx source remove <id>`, since `--account` would replace the account remote they hold; `fail` when the settings cannot be read, with a hint that names the settings file, or, for a file a later agentx wrote, the `settings` row's hint to upgrade agentx. An add that fails takes its own remote back, so what this row names is what no take-back covers: a run killed outright, and a take-back an unrecoverable journal refused. The other direction, an entry whose remote is gone, is not a row: every command that uses the remote names it as missing and `source fetch` writes it back. Reported, never repaired: doctor holds no lock. Emitted only when the `account_repo` row is `ok` and the repo exists |
| `staged_imports` | `ok`, `warn`, `fail` | no `refs/agentx/importing/<run>/<n>` is left behind; a warning names the count of refs, the count of runs they belong to and the first ref, and the hint is the `git update-ref -d` that removes one. An install or an update check killed between its fast-import and the journal that publishes the commits leaves them, and they pin the objects they name. Reported, never swept: the staging refs of a run still fetching are indistinguishable from these (no lock is held while either exists and the run id says nothing about which), so a sweep would have to guess, and a wrong guess takes the objects a live install is about to publish. Emitted only when the `account_repo` row is `ok` and the repo exists |
| `library` | `ok`, `fail` | the library is a writable directory, or does not exist yet; the first install creates it |
| `client:<slug>` | `info` | one row per detected agent configuration, sorted by slug; the detail is `<name>: <configuration directory>` |
| `clients` | `info`, `warn` | `<n> of <m> registered clients detected`, where `m` is the size of the client registry; `warn` with a hint when nothing is detected |

`doctor`, one event per check:

| Field | Type | Meaning |
|---|---|---|
| `check` | string | the check name from the table above |
| `status` | string | `ok`, `warn`, `fail` or `info` |
| `detail` | string | what was found, naming the version, path or error involved |
| `hint` | string | how to fix it; absent when there is nothing to suggest |

Without `--json` the rows print in three titled sections, `System` (`git`, `fork_merges`, `commit_identity`, `home`), `App` (`lock`, `mutations`, `settings`, `account_repo`, `source_remotes`, `staged_imports`, `library`) and `Clients` (every `client:<slug>` row; the `clients` detail is shown after the section title rather than as a row), each row indented as `<glyph> <check>  <status>  <detail>`, the glyph being `✓` for `ok`, `!` for `warn`, `✗` for `fail` and `•` for `info`, with the columns aligned across sections. After the sections, a blank line and either `✓ No issues detected` or an `<n> issues` block (`1 issue`) listing every `warn` and `fail` row again as `<glyph> <check>  <detail>`, with its `hint` on the next line as `hint: <hint>` aligned under the detail. Exit code 2 when git is missing or too old; the run stops after the `git` row, since nothing else can be checked. Exit code 8 when the account repo is unusable, after every row. Otherwise 0, warnings included. The `result` event carries `ok: false` exactly when the exit code is non-zero.

## Account repo

The account repo is `account.git` in agentx home, a bare repository. It is created on first use, under the lock, by the first command that needs it: `agentx source add`, `--account` included, a command that fetches an account remote whose settings entry an import wrote, `agentx skill new`, `agentx skill fork` of an unmanaged skill or a plugin's skill, `agentx skill add` when it adds the source it installs from or fetches it again with `--fetch`, or `agentx adopt` when it adds the source of a skill it adopts. Its creation bumps `version` like any mutation. On a fresh machine it does not exist and `agentx doctor` reports that. Creation runs `git init --bare` in the isolated environment and sets `gc.auto=0` (maintenance runs on the serve child's timer, never inside a command, see [Serve](#serve)), `core.logAllRefUpdates=true` (reflogs, which a bare repository lacks by default) and `merge.conflictStyle=zdiff3`, and, with git 2.48 or newer, `worktree.useRelativePaths=true`, so that a fork's worktree and the account repo name each other with relative paths and survive agentx home being moved as a whole; a repository created by an older git is never switched over, since its first relative worktree would make it one an older git cannot read. The repository is renamed into place only once every step succeeded. A present `account.git` that is not a bare repository git can read is exit code 8 with a hint naming the path.

Git config in the account repo holds only what git owns: the remotes of [Sources](#sources), each `src-<id>`, the [account remote](#account-remote)'s among them, and the tracking configuration of a fork branch, `branch.skills/<name>.remote` naming the account remote's remote, which `agentx skill add` with no source writes so that `git status` in the fork's worktree compares it with its branch there. agentx itself never reads that configuration: it reads a fork's branch on the account remote from `refs/remotes/src-<id>/skills/<name>` by name.

## Sources

A source is a git repository added by URL: skills are installed from a shared source, and the edits of one are published back to it when the skill is named (see **Publishing a skill of a shared source** under [Skills](#skills)), and forks are published to and installed from the [account remote](#account-remote), the one source added with `--account`. `agentx source add <url>` fetches it, `agentx source fetch [<url|id>]... [--all]` fetches sources that are already added, `agentx source list` lists the sources of this machine, `agentx source skills <url|id>` lists the installable skills of a fetched source, and `agentx source remove <url|id>` deletes one. `add`, `fetch` and `remove` are mutations; `list` and `skills` read and never touch the network.

**URL forms.** `source add` and every `<url>` argument accept, with an optional `#<ref>` fragment on each: `owner/repo` and `owner/repo/<subpath>`, which resolve to GitHub; `https://github.com/owner/repo`, with or without `.git` or a trailing slash, followed by an optional `/<subpath>` or `/tree/<ref>/<subpath>`; a GitLab URL, `https://gitlab.com/group/subgroup/repo`, with an optional `/-/tree/<ref>/<subpath>`; the SSH shorthand `[user@]host:path` and `ssh://` and `git://` URLs, with `git+ssh://` and `ssh+git://` read as `ssh://`; any `https://` or `http://` host; `file://<path>`; and an absolute path on disk, `/srv/skills.git`, read as `file:///srv/skills.git`. These are the forms git reads as the URL of a remote; its `<transport>::<address>` remote-helper form and every other scheme are not among them. Everything else, a GitHub `blob`, `pull` or other page URL included, is exit code 1 with a hint listing the forms, and so are `user:password@host/path` without a scheme, which the shorthand would read as the host `user`, and a Windows path such as `C:\skills`. The result is the canonical URL, an optional subpath and an optional ref. The canonical URL is the source's identity: the scheme and host in lower case, the host without a trailing dot and without a port that is the scheme's default, an IP address host in one spelling and bracketed, `.git` and a trailing slash dropped, `git@host:path` written as `ssh://git@host/path` and `host:path` as `ssh://host/path`, the path's percent escapes kept as escapes, and a `file://` path cleaned but otherwise as it is on disk. The canonical URL of anything accepted parses back to itself, to the same id, and to the repository alone with no subpath and no ref: it is printed by `source list` and read back by `source remove` and `source skills`, so a form that did not round trip would name a different source than the one it came from. A pin must be a ref git accepts, which is what `git check-ref-format --allow-onelevel` allows for a branch or tag of one level or several, `v2` and `release/1.x` alike, or a commit id; a name git would refuse is exit code 1 here rather than a fetch failure later. A pin of `HEAD` names the remote's default branch, which an unpinned source follows anyway, so it is no pin. On GitHub the repository is `owner/repo` and what follows is the subpath; elsewhere the repository path ends at `/-/` or at the first segment named `*.git`, and what follows is the subpath, so `https://git.example.com/team/repo.git/skills` and `file:///srv/skills.git/tools` name a subpath and `https://git.example.com/team/repo/skills` is the repository `team/repo/skills`. The ref of a tree URL is the pin, and it is the one path segment after `tree`, as GitHub cannot tell a ref with a slash in it from a ref followed by a subpath: a ref such as `release/1.x` is pinned with `#release/1.x`. A fragment naming a different ref is exit code 1. A ref must be a name git accepts for a branch or tag, or a commit id. The subpath scopes what the command lists and is never stored. Once its percent escapes are decoded it is held to the rule a path in a source's tree is held to (see [Skills](#skills)): a subpath with an empty, `.`, `..` or `.git` component, the last in any case, or holding a backslash or a NUL is exit code 1, however it was escaped, so `skills%2F..` is refused as `skills/..` is. A subpath that is not a directory of the repository at the fetched commit is exit code 5. A user or token embedded in the URL is removed before the URL is used anywhere: it never enters the settings, the account repo's configuration, a trailer or an event, and the command logs one warning saying so and naming the credential helper. For an `ssh://` URL the user name (`git@`) is part of the address and is kept; only a password is removed.

**Pushing.** A source is fetched from and pushed to at its canonical URL, the one URL its entry and its remote `remote.src-<id>.url` hold; agentx records no second URL for pushes. The canonical URL keeps the transport and the host it was given with, and over SSH the user, so an SSH URL is pushed to over SSH, and git rewrites it by the user's own configuration as it does every URL: `url.<base>.insteadOf` for fetches and pushes, `url.<base>.pushInsteadOf` for pushes alone, so a source fetched over HTTPS can be pushed to over SSH. A setting the user keys on a remote's URL with `includeIf "hasconfig:remote.*.url:..."` matches the canonical URL, never the form the source was added with (see **Unattended git** under [Git](#git)). Known limitation: an account remote given in the SSH shorthand with a relative path, `user@host:relative/path`, is canonicalised to an absolute path, `ssh://user@host/relative/path`, which a server that resolves the shorthand against the user's home directory reads as another repository; GitHub and GitLab, whose paths are the same either way, are unaffected.

**The account remote.** The entry with `account: true` is the [account remote](#account-remote): it holds one branch per fork, `skills/<name>`, and is fetched whole, never as a shared source, since a fetch without blobs would leave its forks' history without them. `agentx source add <url> --account` adds it (see **Setting it** under [Account remote](#account-remote)), and a plain `source add` of its URL adds it again, still the account remote. Every other source is a shared source, which holds skills in folders anywhere in the tree of one branch, and installing one reads a managed copy of it. One repository is never both: `source add --account` of a repository a shared source names, by its URL or another, is exit code 6, with the hint to remove that source first with `agentx source remove <url>`; and the account remote holds no skill on one branch to install, so `source add` of a URL whose repository the account remote names is exit code 6, `<url> names the repository of the account remote <account url>` (or `<url> is the account remote` for its own URL), with the hint `agentx skill list --remote` to see its skills, then `agentx skill add --name <name>` to install one; so are `source skills` and `skill add` of the account remote, with or without a `#<ref>`. Two URLs name one repository when their paths are the same as the canonical URL is formed, the scheme, the user and the port left out, so that one repository over HTTPS and over SSH is one, on hosts that are the same after an SSH host is read through the user's SSH configuration, the `hostname` that `ssh -G <host>` prints in the user environment, which opens no connection, and through the second names a forge serves one repository under (`ssh.github.com` and `www.github.com` for `github.com`, `altssh.gitlab.com` for `gitlab.com`, `altssh.bitbucket.org` for `bitbucket.org`); without `ssh` every host is read as itself. The update check of `skill check-updates` and of [serve](#serve), and serve's search index, leave the account remote out.

**Access.** Whether git lets this machine write to a source is found out from git, and recorded in the source's entry as `access`: `writable`, `read-only`, or nothing while it is `unknown`, with `access_checked`, when it was asked. `source add`, `--account` included, `skill add` when it adds or fetches the source, `adopt` and `skill add` with no source when they add one, and `source fetch` for each source it fetched, ask once the fetch has worked, outside the lock, and record the answer in the settings write that ends the run; `source fetch` asks in the worker that fetched the source, so that the checks overlap the other fetches. Nothing else asks: not `skill check-updates`, not the update check of [serve](#serve) and not `import`, which leaves the access out of every source it writes. The check pushes nothing: it is `git push --dry-run --porcelain --no-signed --no-verify --no-recurse-submodules --no-follow-tags agentx :refs/heads/agentx-access-check`, a dry run of deleting a branch no source holds, with `-c push.negotiate=false`, `-c push.pushOption=`, which clears any push option the user sets for their own pushes and a server that takes none would refuse, and `-c core.hooksPath=/dev/null`, in the user environment, through a remote `agentx` given the source's URL on the command line, so that the user's URL rewrites, `pushInsteadOf` among them, apply as they would to a push. It needs no object and sends no pack, so it answers for an empty repository, and runs from an empty throwaway repository rather than the account repo, so that the user's configuration applies as it does to this source alone (see **Unattended git** under [Git](#git)). `writable` is git's status line for that ref, and exit 0. Over a path, `file://` or a directory on disk, where a dry run checks no permission, `writable` also needs this machine to be allowed to write the repository's git directory, its `objects` and its `refs/heads`, or its `reftable` for a repository that keeps its refs there, found the way git finds the repository for a push; one it may not write is `read-only`. `read-only` is a strong claim: it needs one of the lines git and the hosts agentx knows answer a user without write rights with, such as `Permission to <repo> denied`, `You are not allowed to push code`, a deploy key `marked as read only`, an archived repository, gitolite's `DENIED by` or a git daemon's `access denied or repository not exported`. Everything else is `unknown`: a host that wants the user's token or key authorised for an organisation's single sign-on, or an IP allow list, which the user can fix, a 403 that names no denial, a credential that was refused or not available without asking, a host not trusted yet, and a server that could not be reached or did not answer within 30 seconds. The single sign-on and IP allow list answers print a `warning:` naming the source and the host's line, with `authorise your token or key for the organisation, then run 'agentx source fetch <url>'`.
