An unpinned shared source is also asked, after the check and as unattended, which branch its `HEAD` names, with `git ls-remote --symref agentx HEAD` from a throwaway repository as the check is, recorded as `default_branch` when it names one a pin may name; a pinned source keeps the one its entry has. Neither can fail the command: an answer that does not come leaves the access `unknown` and the default branch as it was, and a stop during either stops the run as any stop does. `access` is advice, not a rule: rights change on the server, and no command refuses on a recorded `read-only`. A `writable` answer does not prove that a push will be taken: a dry run evaluates no branch protection, ruleset or hook of the server's, a plain SSH server may only find out what a key may do once receive-pack runs, and over a path the server's hooks still run.

**Id.** The source id is the first 16 lowercase hex characters of SHA-256 over the canonical URL. It names the remote `src-<id>` and the ref `refs/agentx/sources/<id>` in the account repo and is accepted wherever a URL is. An id that names no source entry of the settings is exit code 5 from every command that takes one, `source fetch`, `source skills`, `source remove` and `skill add`, with a hint to list the sources, since an id is a hash and names no URL that could be fetched; `source remove` still clears what the account repo holds under an id no entry names, a remote an interrupted add left behind. The one exception is an id a managed skill's lineage still records, which is a source this machine removed, or never added, while a skill installed from it stays: its URL is known, so the message names it and the hint is `agentx source add <url>` with the URL the lineage records, sanitised and quoted as the lines [import](#export-and-import) prints are, and without a pin, the pin having gone with the entry. The lineage is read for that refusal alone, in one `for-each-ref`, and an account repo git cannot read leaves the refusal as it is without it. A removal that takes such a skill off the machine, `skill remove` without `--from` or with `--from universal`, deletes its import branch and with it that record, while `--from <configuration>` keeps both; once no managed skill records the URL, the id is refused as any other is, with the hint to list the sources.

**Fetch.** `source add` opens the account repo, creating it when needed, then writes the remote in the isolated environment: `remote.src-<id>.url` is the canonical URL, `remote.src-<id>.fetch` is `+<pin>:refs/agentx/fetching/<id>`, or `+HEAD:refs/agentx/fetching/<id>` for an unpinned source so that the remote's default branch is followed whatever its name; its left side records the pin and its right side is never the source ref, so that `refs/agentx/sources/<id>` is written by the `update-ref` that ends a whole fetch and by nothing else – not by a fetch agentx did not make, and not by a refspec an interrupted run left behind, `remote.src-<id>.tagOpt=--no-tags`, `remote.src-<id>.promisor=true` and `remote.src-<id>.partialclonefilter=blob:none`. Each key is written with `--replace-all`, so that one a user gave a second value holds one again. It then runs `git fetch --quiet --no-tags --no-write-fetch-head --recurse-submodules=no --no-show-forced-updates --refmap= --filter=blob:none src-<id> +<pin>:refs/agentx/fetching/<run>/<id>` in the user environment, `<run>` a random name each fetch picks for itself. A fetch lands on a **staging ref** of its own and never on the source ref: filling a source takes two network round trips, and between them there is a commit the account repo can read whose blobs are not here yet, which no reader of a source ref may ever see. The refspec is built from the pin the settings hold rather than left to `remote.src-<id>.fetch`, so that a remote a run was interrupted before it could write cannot make a fetch follow a ref the settings do not name; `--refmap=` is what makes it the only refspec, since one on the command line does not replace the configured one: git also updates that one opportunistically. The remote's configuration is written under the lock, since `git config` fails rather than waits when another command holds the repository's own config lock; the fetch itself runs outside it, so that a slow network never blocks a scan. For that reason every fetch that filters, this one and the batch below, carries `-c remote.src-<id>.promisor=true -c remote.src-<id>.partialclonefilter=blob:none` before `fetch`: git writes those two settings into the config itself, and moves `core.repositoryformatversion` to 1, when a filtered fetch goes through a remote whose config lacks them, such as one `source remove` took away while the fetch ran, and that write could fail another command's `git config` and leave a remote with no URL behind. An add that does not reach its settings entry takes that remote back: a source the settings did not name before loses its remote, its source ref and its staging ref altogether, and one they did name goes back to the pin they still hold, its source ref back to the commit it held before the add's fetch unless another fetch has moved it since, so that the account repo never keeps a remote for a source the settings do not name – nothing would name such a remote again, `source fetch` and `source skills` both answering from the settings. The cleanup runs under the hold the failing write already has, since a run that had to win the lock back would be competing for it with whatever just made it fail; only a run that never took the lock at all can leave the remote behind, and the next `source add` or `source fetch` of the same source brings it back in line. Once the entry is written the remote belongs to it and stays, whatever else the run still fails at. The fetch brings every commit and tree of the ref and no blob; a server that does not support partial clone ignores the filter, which git reports as a warning, and the fetch is a full one. A pin that names no branch, tag or commit of the source is exit code 5; any other fetch failure, unreachable host, authentication, or not a repository, is exit code 3 with the first line of git's error and a hint naming the credential helper, worded for the dropped credential when the URL carried one. The staging ref is then read as a commit (an annotated tag is peeled; the object the ref names, tag or commit, is what the source ref will end up holding), and the skills under the subpath are listed from the trees, tree objects only: every directory holding a `SKILL.md` (a regular file; a symlink does not count), the root included, skipping any directory below the subpath whose name starts with `.` or is `node_modules`, at any depth, while a skill inside another skill's directory counts. The `SKILL.md` blobs are then fetched in one batch, by object id on the standard input of one `git fetch` carrying `-c fetch.negotiationAlgorithm=noop`, the flags above except `--no-show-forced-updates`, and `--refmap=`, `--filter=blob:none` and `--stdin`, the way git itself fills a partial clone; the batch names objects rather than refs and updates none, so it has no forced update to suppress; a server that serves the filtered fetch but refuses single objects gets one `git fetch --refetch --no-filter` with the same flags instead, which makes the fetch a full one. The batch covers every `SKILL.md` of the repository, the skipped directories included, not only those under this command's subpath: a subpath scopes one listing and is never stored, so every later listing, of any part of the source, is answered from the account repo without the network. No blob is ever fetched one at a time: every local read runs with `GIT_NO_LAZY_FETCH=1`. Every `SKILL.md` of the commit is then looked for in one `git cat-file --batch-check`; a batch a server answered without sending every object it was asked for is exit code 5 with a hint to fetch the source again, the source ref unmoved. The blobs are read in one `cat-file --batch`, and the skill list (subpath, name, description, tree id) is built in memory and never written to disk.

**Publishing a fetch.** Only once the commit is whole does `git update-ref refs/agentx/sources/<id> <fetched object>` move the source ref onto it, followed by one `git update-ref --stdin` that deletes the fetch's staging ref and `refs/agentx/fetching/<id>`, the ref the configured refspec names, which only the full fetch that the blob batch of an install or an update check falls back to writes, and nothing reads. `refs/agentx/sources/<id>` therefore never names a commit whose `SKILL.md` blobs are not in the account repo, and a fetch is invisible to every other reader – the [serve](#serve) child rebuilding its source index, a concurrent `source skills` or `source list`, the next command after a crash – until it is whole: each of them sees the commit the last complete fetch left, then the new one, and nothing in between. A fetch that fails at any step, for any cause, leaves the source ref exactly where it was, so a failed fetch never costs a machine the source it already had. The staging ref is deleted when the fetch ends, whether it finished or failed; only a killed process can leave one behind, and nothing reads it. No later fetch can tell the staging ref of a fetch that was killed from that of one still running, so a leftover stays until `source remove` deletes it with every other staging ref of the source. Finally, under the lock, the settings entry is written: `url`, `pin` when set, `last_fetched` now, and what the check of **Access** above found, `access`, `access_checked` and, for an unpinned source, `default_branch`; an existing entry for the URL is replaced, and every field the add does not find out again is kept: `alias`, `default_branch` when the add did not read one, and `access` and `access_checked` together unless the add checked the access again, which it does unless a stop cut the check short. Adding a source again therefore re-fetches it and changes its pin to what the new command said, a missing fragment included.

**A remote the settings do not name is not a source.** `source fetch` and `source skills` both answer from the settings, so a remote no entry names is a remote nothing will ever name again or clean up. It exists in the account repo only because an entry names it, or because an add is in flight for it. An add that wrote the remote and cannot write the entry therefore takes the remote back: a source that was not there before goes altogether, remote and ref, and one that was there goes back to the pin the settings still hold, which is what the next command will read. An add at another pin also puts the source ref back on the commit that pin fetched, so that `source skills` never lists a commit the settings do not follow; an add at the pin the settings hold leaves the ref on what it fetched, a later commit of the branch they follow, as `source fetch` would, so that running the add again does not move the ref again and have the [serve](#serve) child list the source again under the shared lock just as that run wants the lock. The same take-back answers a failed fetch, which also leaves a remote written and nothing recording it. It runs under the lock: where the settings write held the lock, inside that same hold, and where the settings write never won it, as a [take-back](#lock-and-version-file) that waits for the lock rather than giving up on it. The run still ends with the exit code of whatever stopped it, a lost lock still being code 7. A take-back that fails even then is reported rather than passed over: the message names the remote left in the account repo and says no source names it, and the hint gives the two repairs – `agentx source add <url>` again, which rewrites the remote and writes the entry, or `agentx source remove <id>`, which clears the remote. A take-back is itself a mutation, so an earlier mutation's journal that cannot be recovered refuses it exactly as it refuses any other command, whatever the take-back waits for: the remote then stays in the account repo, reported the same way, until the journal is resolved and the add is run again. The first hold of the lock, the one that writes the remote, is not a take-back and gives up like any other mutation: a run that loses it has written nothing.

**Fetch again.** `agentx source fetch [<url|id>]... [--all]` fetches sources that are already added, so that a source no skill was installed from, which the update check of `agentx skill check-updates` does not fetch, can be brought up to date now rather than on the next tick of the [serve](#serve) child, which refreshes every source: several arguments fetch several sources and `--all` fetches every source of the machine. Neither an argument nor `--all` is exit code 1 naming both forms, and so is giving both at once. Every argument is resolved against the settings before anything is fetched: a URL that names no added source is exit code 5 with a hint to add it and an id that names none answers as under **Id.** above; a subpath in a URL argument is ignored, since a fetch covers the whole source whatever part of it is named, and a `#<ref>` other than the entry's pin is exit code 1 naming the stored pin, the rule `source skills` follows; a source named twice, by URL and by id, is fetched once. Each source is fetched at the pin the settings hold, through the remote `source add` configured, by the fetch above and nothing else: the blobless fetch onto the staging ref, then the `SKILL.md` blobs of the whole repository in one batch, then the source ref. The update check fetches the sources it covers the same way, with the same early hold of the lock, the same parallelism and the same `last_fetched` write at the end, beside the refs it records (see **Checking for updates** under [Skills](#skills)), and the one [serve](#serve) runs on its timer covers every source of the settings. Each fetch stages on a ref of its own, `refs/agentx/fetching/<run>/<id>`, so no two fetches ever share one: not the parallel fetches of one run, and not two commands that fetch the same source at once, the update check of the [serve](#serve) child among them. A source that fails leaves its own source ref where it was without disturbing any other. A fetch writes the remote only to bring it back in line with the settings: the remotes of the run's sources are read in one `git config --get-regexp` under the early lock, and one whose URL, refspec or promisor settings are not what the entry wants is written again whole, which a run interrupted between the remote and the settings leaves behind. A shared source with no remote at all is left alone rather than given half of one; the account remote's remote is written whenever it is missing, as settings an import wrote leave it, since its forks need nothing else to be fetched. That changes no pin: it is what the settings say, and only `source add` sets it. An account repo that does not exist is exit code 5 with a hint to add the source when a shared source is named, nothing having been fetched on this machine at all, and is created for a run that names the account remote alone, and so is a source whose remote the account repo no longer holds, which an interrupted removal leaves: git answers a fetch through a remote that is not there by naming that remote, which is agentx's own name for the source, and the run says what `source skills` says of the same state instead. That hint is `agentx source add <url>[#<pin>]` with the pin the settings hold, the argument sanitised and quoted as the lines [import](#export-and-import) prints are: this is the state an import leaves, and `source add` writes the pin its argument names, so the bare URL would unpin the source. The sources are fetched in parallel, at most four at a time as the handshakes of a scan are, outside the lock, and each one ends with a `progress` event of phase `fetch` carrying its URL. A source that could not be fetched is one warning naming it and the reason, and the run goes on: every other source still lands. The lock is taken once before the network, to recover an unfinished [mutation journal](#mutation-journal) and to refuse a run that could not write the settings anyway, and once at the end for one write of `last_fetched`, now, and of what the access check found (see **Access** above), for every source that fetched. A source removed while the run fetched has no entry to write, is left out of the report rather than named as present and fresh, and has the ref its fetch published taken away again under that same lock, so that a removal a fetch raced still leaves nothing of the source. That write bumps `version` like any mutation, so the [serve](#serve) child rebuilds its source index after a fetch that moved a ref, and a fetch that moved nothing still updates `last_fetched`. One run over several sources has one exit code, and it is the code every failure agrees on: fetching one source answers exactly as adding or listing that source would, and a run whose sources all failed the same way answers for that cause. Exit code 3, the source-level refusal, is what a run whose causes disagree answers, no one code being true of them all. The `error` and `result` events name every source that failed, the `hint` answers the `code`, and each failure is one `warning:` line on stderr naming its source. `--all` on a machine with no source prints what `source list` prints for none and exits 0. The account remote is fetched as its own commands fetch it (see **Fetching** under [Account remote](#account-remote)), whole, after the shared sources of the run; its access is checked and recorded as a shared source's is, and its `source` event carries `forks` instead of `commit`.

**Listing.** `source skills` resolves its argument to an entry of the settings, exit code 5 when there is none, with a hint to add it for a URL argument and the hint under **Id.** for an id; a URL argument may carry a subpath, and a fragment naming a ref other than the entry's pin is exit code 1. It reads `refs/agentx/sources/<id>` (exit code 5 when the account repo holds no ref for the source, with the hint `source fetch` gives that state, and exit code 5 with a hint to fetch the source again when an object the listing needs is missing) and lists the skills as above from the account repo alone. A local git failure in any `source` command is exit code 8, and so is a `source fetch` run over several sources when every source failed that way; it is never reported as a source that was not fetched: the account repo holding no ref for the source is exit code 5, git failing to read the refs at all is exit code 8, and each hint leads to the command that addresses that cause. `source list` reads the settings and runs two git processes: the `rev-parse --is-bare-repository` that checks the account repo, then one `for-each-ref` over `refs/agentx/sources/`, and one more `for-each-ref` for the account remote over its remote-tracking fork branches.

**Removal.** `source remove` deletes the remote (`git remote remove src-<id>`), then the source ref and every staging ref of the source a killed fetch can have left, `refs/agentx/fetching/<id>` and each `refs/agentx/fetching/<run>/<id>`, which one `git for-each-ref` lists, in one `git update-ref --stdin`, then the settings entry, all under the lock, so that nothing of the source is left under `refs/agentx`. The git state goes first: were the entry deleted first and the git deletion then failed, nothing would name the source any more and its remote would be unreachable for good. The objects stay until maintenance reclaims them. A managed skill installed from the source keeps its coordinates, its import branch, its library directory and its placements, and is `source removed` in the `drift` of its [`library_skill`](#event-types) event and of its snapshot entry for as long as the settings hold no entry for that canonical URL: any command that writes the entry again ends it, whether `source add` of the same canonical URL, an install that names the source by URL, an [`agentx adopt`](#adoption) that adds it, or an [`agentx import`](#export-and-import) of settings that hold it. Any pin clears it, since the canonical URL and not the pin is the source's identity. Adding the source again changes nothing of the skills installed from it: once `source add` has added it, `skill list` is what it was before the removal, byte for byte. `source add`, an install and an adoption fetch the source anew; an import writes the entry alone, which leaves a source not fetched. The account remote is removed as **Setting it** under [Account remote](#account-remote) says, and prints its line.

Without `--json`: `source add` prints `✓ added <url> at <short commit>: <n> skills` for a source that was not there before, and for one that was, `✓ re-fetched <url>, now at <short commit>, was <short commit>`, or `, already at <short commit>` when the fetch changed nothing, so a repeat says what moved rather than repeating that it exists; ` pinned to <ref>` follows the URL when pinned, ` under <subpath>` follows the count when scoped, and every line ends with what the access check found, `; you can write to it`, `; read-only for you` or `; write access unknown`; `source fetch` prints one of those `re-fetched` lines per shared source that fetched and `✓ re-fetched <url>: <n> forks` with what the access check found for the account remote, in the order the command named them, its arguments in their order and `--all` in the order `source list` prints, whatever order the fetches themselves finished in, and nothing for a source that failed, which is a `warning:` line on stderr instead; `source list` prints `<n> sources` and one row per source, `<url>[ (account)]  <access>  <pin, else the default branch, else (unpinned); skills/* for the account remote>  <short commit, <n> forks for the account remote, or not fetched>  <last_fetched>  <id>`, or `No sources. Add one with agentx source add <url>.`; `source skills` prints `<n> skills in <url>[ under <subpath>] at <short commit>` and one row per skill, `<name>  <subpath or .>  <description>`, the three of them sanitised as [Streams](#streams) prescribes, since the source writes all three; `source remove` prints `✓ removed <url>`. A short commit is its first seven characters. Every row of the `source list` and `source skills` listings is indented two spaces; the `re-fetched` lines of `source fetch` are one per source and are not indented, being confirmations rather than rows of a listing.


## Skills

The library is this machine's directory of skills managed by agentx, `AGENTX_LIBRARY` or `~/.agents/skills`. `agentx skill add <source>[/<subpath>] [--name <name>]... [--all] [--except <name>]... [--to <configuration>]... [--copy] [--fetch]` installs skills of a source into it and places them in the configurations that should see them, and `agentx skill add [--name <name>]... [--all] [--except <name>]... [--to <configuration>]... [--copy]`, with no source, installs your own skills from the account remote (see **Installing from the account remote** under [Account remote](#account-remote)); `agentx skill place <name> [--to <configuration>]... [--copy] [--force]` places a skill the library already holds and puts back its `missing` and `displaced` placements; `agentx skill remove <name> [--from <configuration>|universal]... [--remote]` takes a skill out of one configuration or off the machine, and a fork off the account remote too; `agentx skill rename <old> <new>` renames one of your own skills on this machine, as a fork under the new name that keeps its fork id and the removal of the old one, and the next publish renames it on the account remote; `agentx skill diff <name> [--update] [--commit <id>]` shows how a managed skill differs from its base version, or what its update does to that version, and a fork's unpublished edits; `agentx skill check-updates` looks for newer upstream versions of the managed skills and records what it finds; `agentx skill update <name>|--all` applies the update a check found to a managed skill, or to every one that has an update, merging it with the edits of one that was edited; `agentx skill new <name> [--description <text>]` creates a skill of your own; `agentx skill publish [<name>] [-m <message>]` records the edits of one of your own skills, or of every one, and pushes them to the account remote, or pushes the edits of the named skill of a shared source to that source; `agentx skill history <name>` lists a fork's commits; `agentx skill list` reports what the library holds. `add`, `place`, `remove`, `rename`, `check-updates`, `update`, `new`, `fork` and `publish` are mutations; `diff`, `history` and `list` read the library and write nothing to it, and `check-updates` writes nothing to it either.

**A skill the library already holds.** The argument of `skill add` is a source in one of the [URL forms](#sources) or a source id, and anything else is exit code 1 with a hint listing the forms. When that argument is exactly the name of a skill the library holds, one `skill place` would take, whether agentx installed it or not, the refusal is still exit code 1 but says what was meant: the message is `<name> is already in the library; skill add installs a skill from a source` and the hint is `to place it in more clients, run 'agentx skill place <name>'`, which places it into every enabled configuration. The hint carries each `--to` the command line gave, as `--to <id>` in the order given, and `--copy` when `--copy` was given. `<name>` and each `<id>` are quoted for a shell, and a name that starts with `-` comes last after `--`, as in the commands of the warning for a kept copy under **Placements**, so the hint runs as it is printed. A source form wins over a library name: an argument that parses as a source, or that is a source id, is installed from as a source even when the library holds a skill of that exact name, so no library directory changes what an install of a source does. The refusal comes after the flag checks of **Selecting several** and reads the library alone: nothing is fetched, locked or written, and no source is added.

**What is installed.** A source URL the settings hold no entry for is added first, by the steps `agentx source add` takes and nothing else: the remote is written, the source is fetched without blobs and then its `SKILL.md` blobs, outside the lock, and the settings entry is written as a mutation of its own, so an install that fails afterwards keeps the source. The install then reads the listing that fetch built, so the source is fetched once. An id that names no source is exit code 5 with a hint to list the sources: an id names no URL to fetch. An id a managed skill's lineage records, the id of a source this machine removed, is exit code 5 as well, with the hint `agentx source add <url>` naming the URL the lineage records (see [Sources](#sources)); an install that names that source by its URL instead adds it again as above, which ends the `source removed` of every skill installed from it. A source already added is not fetched again unless `--fetch` is given, which fetches it again at the pin the settings hold, exactly as `source add` of that URL and pin would, before the install. Otherwise the account repo must hold its ref, else exit code 5 with a hint to add it, the one `source fetch` gives that state, which names the pin the settings hold even when the argument leaves it out. The version installed is the one the source ref names in the clone: an install resolves the pin the settings hold, or the remote's default branch, against what the last fetch brought, and never reaches for the network to decide what to install nor reads a working copy. The skill's upstream commit is the last commit reachable from that commit that touched the skill's directory, and that commit itself for a skill at the repository root: a commit elsewhere in the source, or a fetch at another time, leaves the upstream commit of an unchanged skill where it was, so two machines that fetched the source at different commits, or one machine before and after a fetch, write the same import commit for the same version. A `#<ref>` other than the stored pin is exit code 1 naming the stored pin, with a hint to change it with `source add <url>#<ref>`, as it is for `source skills`. The skills are those the coordinate and the selection name: a source or a subpath that holds exactly one skill needs no `--name`, one that holds several is exit code 1 with a hint listing their names, a `--name` that names none of them is exit code 5 with the same hint, and a coordinate that holds no skill at all is exit code 5 with a hint to list the source.

**Selecting several.** `--name` may be given once for each skill to install, and a name given twice installs that skill once. `--all` selects every skill the coordinate holds and `--except <name>`, once for each, leaves some of them out. The two forms do not mix: `--all` with `--name` is exit code 1, since `--all` already names everything, and `--except` without `--all` is exit code 1, since excepting from a list one wrote out is a contradiction. Both are refused before anything else happens, so such a command line adds no source. A selection of several skills from a source URL the settings do not hold adds that source first, once, like a selection of one. `--name` and `--except` match a skill's frontmatter `name` case-insensitively, or its directory name when the frontmatter has none, which is what the vercel skills CLI accepts; when two skills share a name, `--name` takes the first in listing order and `--except` leaves out all of them. An `--except` that names no skill of the source is exit code 5 with the same hint a bad `--name` gets, since the run would otherwise install more than was asked for, and an `--except` that leaves nothing to install is exit code 1. The skills are installed in the order the source lists them, which is by subpath, whatever order the flags named them in, so two runs of one selection do the same things in the same order. With no source, `--name` and `--all` select from the account remote instead, by the rules of **Installing from the account remote** under [Account remote](#account-remote).

**What is read.** The commit's whole tree, the objects of the selected skills' trees the account repo does not hold yet and the upstream commit of every selected skill with its committer time are reads that do not depend on each other and run at once; each skill's own entries are taken out of that one listing rather than read again, so a run of thirty skills costs the reads of one. The upstream commit of each skill is the commit `git log -1 --no-renames <commit> -- :(literal)<subpath>` names for that skill alone, under git's default history simplification: a merge whose skill directory matches one of its parents' is not a change, and the history is followed down the first such parent to the commit that changed the directory, so a merge that brought an older change back onto a line that predates it names that change and not itself. The upstream commits of the whole run come out of one walk of the source's history, `git log --full-history --sparse --no-renames --diff-merges=first-parent --raw -t` from the source's commit over the selected subpaths, which git matches literally rather than as patterns: it prints every commit with its parents and what each subpath's entry becomes against the first parent, and agentx follows each subpath down that history as `git log -1` would, so the commit found for a skill does not depend on which other skills the run selected. A skill at the repository root is one more read, of that commit alone. The walk compares trees alone and never reads a blob, which the account repo does not hold. The blobs that are missing across the whole selection, which is every file but the `SKILL.md` a fetch brought, are fetched from the source's remote in one batch by object id, in the user environment, the way a listing fills a partial clone; a version whose blobs are all here already, which is how the same version installed twice looks, costs no network at all. That batch names objects and no ref, so it carries no `--refmap=`, which git refuses outright without a refspec on the same command line. A blob the batch did not bring is not reported as missing but kills the read that asks for it, the account repo being a partial clone, so the objects the run still does not hold are asked of `rev-list` and left out of a second read: a version whose files did not arrive costs its own skill and not the run. The files of every selected skill are then read in one `cat-file --batch` and each [content hash](#content-hash) is computed in process, over the frontmatter name and description and the files as the tree holds them: the version is never laid out on disk to be hashed. An entry that is neither a regular file nor a directory, a symlink or a submodule, is left out of the import with one warning naming it, since the import tree holds files alone; the content hash covers what was imported, so the library directory and the hash always agree. Every path of a selected skill's tree is checked before any of it is read or written, since the source's own bytes name where a file is laid out: an entry whose path is empty or absolute, holds a backslash or a NUL, or has an empty, `.`, `..` or `.git` component, the last in any case, refuses that skill with exit code 6 naming the entry, and nothing of it is written. git never checks such an entry out, but a fetch does not refuse one. The other skills of the source stay installable, and a listing leaves out a directory whose own path is such a path.

**The import commit.** One parentless commit per upstream version, written over the objects the source already put in the account repo. Its tree holds exactly one entry, a directory named after the upstream's own directory, the last segment of the subpath or the repository name for a skill at the root, and under it the skill's tree, with modes `100644` and `100755`, every tree of it as git writes one today. A directory the source already stores that way is reused whole; the rest are written anew: a directory holding an entry that had to be left out, a directory above one written anew, and a directory the source stores with a mode git reads but no longer writes, such as `100664` or a directory mode padded with a zero. git lists such a mode as the canonical one, but the tree keeps an id no directory on disk has, so a skill reused whole from it would never read as `current`. The id every tree ends at is computed in process first, as the `state` of a `library_skill` event computes a directory's, and every id git answers with is held to it, so the tree an import commit holds is the one a library directory holding the version is compared with. Every entry name is the source's own bytes. A source is any repository a user adds, so a name that git's line-based formats would quote (one starting with a double quote, which git C-unquotes when it reads it back, or one holding a newline, which ends a record) is carried through unchanged: tree entries are read and written NUL-terminated, never one per line. The import tree of a version whose entries an import keeps therefore matches the source's tree name for name, and the [content hash](#content-hash) names the tree the commit holds. Its author and committer are `agentx <agentx@localhost>` and both dates are the upstream commit's committer time as epoch seconds with `+0000`. Its subject is `import <canonical URL>[/<subpath>] at <first 12 of the upstream commit> content <first 12 of the content hash>` and its trailers are `Agentx-Source`, the canonical URL, `Agentx-Path`, the subpath or `.` at the repository root, `Agentx-Upstream-Commit` and `Agentx-Content-Hash`. There are four trailers and no more: nothing about a machine, a fork or an account enters an import commit, so two machines that accept the same upstream version write the same commit id, whatever their time zone, git configuration, machine id or paths. A reader refuses a message whose `Agentx-Source` is not the canonical URL of a source, which is what tells a source from a plugin coordinate such as `<name>@<marketplace>`, whose path leaves the repository, whose commit is not an object id or whose hash is not a content hash. It refuses a message carrying a fifth `Agentx-` trailer as well, rather than ignoring the extra one: a message that carries anything else about a machine, a fork or an account was not written as an import commit, and reading it as one would credit a version to a commit that does not hold it. Nothing is written that this reader would not read back unchanged: the message an import commit is about to carry is read with that same reader first, whichever command writes it, and a version whose lineage it refuses, or hands back naming anything other than what was written, is refused instead of recorded. The two sides of an import commit are therefore one rule and not two that have to be kept in step. A trailer that is not agentx's, `Signed-off-by` among them, is ignored. Trailers are read from the last paragraph of the message alone, as git reads them, and a message of one paragraph has none; a fork's tip is read for lineage on every listing while its message is the user's own, so a body that quotes an `Agentx-Source` line is never read as lineage. Every commit of a run is written through one `git fast-import`, however many skills the run installs, each one pointing at a tree that is already in the object store: the trees are built first, one `git mktree -z --batch` per level of the deepest skill and one for the roots of all of them, every record NUL-terminated so that a name git would quote in a line-based format arrives as the source's own bytes, and the stream names the tree rather than the files, so nothing about how a run is batched can reach a commit id. One writer writes every commit, whatever the run installs, which is what makes a skill installed alone and the same skill installed among thirty end at the same id. Where `git commit-tree` accepts the same version it writes the same commit byte for byte, but it does not accept every one: it refuses `0 +0000` and any epoch that does not fit in 32 bits, both of which `fast-import` takes. An upstream committer time is refused here only when it is not a decimal integer git could have written, so what installs is what git can date. Until the mutation points the import branches at them, the commits are held by one staging ref each under `refs/agentx/importing/<run>/<n>`, a namespace of that run's own, so that the objects stay reachable while the journal is written and two runs never share a ref; the refs are deleted in one transaction when the run ends, and a run that is killed leaves them, holding nothing the account repo would not keep anyway.

**The library directory** is materialised under the skill's frontmatter `name`, falling back to the upstream directory name when it has none, which is the name `source skills` lists it under. A name agentx cannot hold as both a library directory and an import branch is exit code 6: renaming on install is not offered. That is a name that is empty, hidden or carries a separator, which no directory of the library can be, and a name git refuses as one level of a ref, which is one carrying a control character, a space or any of `~^:?*[\`, one holding `..` or `@{`, `@` by itself, or one ending in `.` or `.lock`. Both are checked before anything is written: the name comes out of the source's frontmatter, and a name only one of the two accepted would fail in the middle of the mutation, leaving a journal every later command would recover and fail on; the branch is refused here rather than on the `update-ref`, where the whole run's ref transaction would go with it. The content is built in a hidden directory beside it and read back as a scan would read it, and only a directory whose content hash is the version's is renamed into place. If the library already holds that name: the same content hash is adopted, which writes the import branch and copies nothing, and different content is exit code 6 with a hint to remove it or fork it. A dangling symlink into the agentx worktrees directory counts as absent. A library this machine cannot read or write is exit code 6 with a hint naming it and pointing at `agentx doctor`, whose `library` row says the same: unlike a placement, the library is not optional, since without it there is nothing to place.

**The import branch** `refs/heads/managed/<name>` is created with an expected old value of empty, every branch of a run in one `git update-ref --stdin` and so in one transaction, the updates wrapped in `start` and `commit`: a run writes all of its branches or none of them, and a stream that never reaches its `commit`, because the writer died part way through it, writes none rather than the prefix git had read. A branch already at this commit is the same version installed again and is left where it is. A branch at another commit is exit code 6, an install not being an update, and a name taken by a fork is exit code 6 too.

**Placements.** The explicit targets are every enabled configuration; `--to <configuration>` replaces them for that install, whatever their enabled state, and an id that names no detected configuration is exit code 5 with a hint listing the detected ones. Installing into the library exposes the skill to every client that reads the universal location whatever these choices are. A configuration whose client reads the library as one of its own skills directories, or one of whose skills directories leads to the library directory through a symlink, or the library to it, `reads_library` in a snapshot, is a universal client: the library entry is its placement and no symlink is made, since a second entry would make that client list the skill twice; the placement is recorded at the library path. A universal client sees every skill of the library, enabled or not and named by `--to` or not, and that is intended: the targets decide which other clients get a skill. So an install, and `skill place` after it, names every universal client detected on this machine, in the `universal` field of each `library_skill` event, at the end of the `result` and in the text below; a machine with none names none. For every other configuration a placement is a symlink at `<the client's own skills directory>/<name>` pointing at the library directory, and the directory is created when it does not exist. If the placement path already exists: a symlink naming the library directory, dangling or not, is already right and is left alone; a real directory that is the library directory itself, which a library entry made a symlink to that path leaves there, is already the placement: the client reads the library's own directory, so it is left as it is and reported as placed, never adopted, its row in the text showing the mode `library` as its `placements` entry does, and with `--copy` it is no copy and `copy_mode` records nothing for it; any other real directory whose content hash is this version's is replaced by a symlink and reported as adopted, its content retained until the outcome is verified; anything else is skipped with a warning naming the path, a symlink somewhere other than the library included however its target reads, since that is the user's link to the user's directory, and the `result` names how many placements were skipped. A placement this machine cannot make is skipped and counted the same way: a skills directory it cannot write, one on a read-only mount, one a platform privacy rule refuses, or a path that is not a directory at all. One client agentx cannot reach never costs the run the library directory, the import branch or any other placement. Whether the directory can hold the placement is settled before the journal is written, not when the step runs, so a client directory agentx cannot use leaves no mutation for a later command to trip over. `--copy` makes that install's placements copies of the library directory instead of symlinks, replacing a symlink that named the library, and records the skill and those configurations in `copy_mode` of the [machine settings](#machine-settings) in one write for the whole install. A real directory of this version at the placement path, other than the library directory itself, is adopted with `--copy` too, and reported as adopted: nothing is written and only `copy_mode` changes, but the directory becomes one a removal deletes, which the run has to say. A configuration `copy_mode` already records for the skill gets a copy whether or not `--copy` is given, from an install, `skill place` and `config enable --place-all` alike, so a placement never becomes a symlink while `copy_mode` still names it a copy; a copy of this version already at that placement path is left as the placement and is not reported as adopted, since agentx made it. A real directory there that does not hold this version is still that copy, the one a removal deletes, and is kept byte for byte and skipped, since replacing it would take what it holds with it; it is counted with the skipped placements and is a `warning:` of its own, two lines on stderr: `warning: <id>'s copy of <name> is different from the library, so it was left unchanged (<path>)`, then, indented by two spaces, `to replace it with the library version: agentx skill remove <name> --from <id> && agentx skill place <name> --to <id> --copy`. `<path>` is printed as every other warning prints a placement path. With `--json` it is one `log` event of level `warn`, whose message is the two lines joined by `; `: `<id>'s copy of <name> is different from the library, so it was left unchanged (<path>); to replace it with the library version: agentx skill remove <name> --from <id> && agentx skill place <name> --to <id> --copy`. The first `<name>` is the name, never quoted. In the two commands `<name>` is printed as it is when it is made of letters, digits and `._/:@%+=,#-` alone and does not start with `#`, and otherwise in single quotes, each `'` in it written `'\''`, as `import` prints the argument of the command it names, so that a POSIX shell reads it back as that one argument; a name that starts with `-` comes last in both commands, after the flags and `--`, as in `agentx skill remove --from <id> -- <name>` and `agentx skill place --to <id> --copy -- <name>`, since agentx would otherwise read it as a flag. Both lines are sanitised as [Streams](#streams) prescribes for every line on stderr, the quoted name included, so for a name holding a control character or a run of spaces the two commands are exact only in the message of the JSON `log` event. A `log` event carries no hint, so the way on is part of the message. The warning says only that the copy is different from the library, never how it came to differ: nothing records what a copy held when it was placed, so an edit of the copy and a change to the library directory since look the same. Following it gives the configuration the library's version as a copy again: the removal deletes the recorded copy, what it holds included, and takes the configuration out of `copy_mode`, and the placement with `--copy` records it there again, since `copy_mode` named it for a client that may not follow a symlink.

**The mutation.** The refs, the library directories, the placements and the one settings write are one journaled mutation under the exclusive lock, in that order, with every input read again under the lock and nothing live changed before the journal that describes all of it is on disk. A run of thirty skills is one bigger journal and not thirty journals, so an interrupted run is finished in one recovery rather than left between two of them. The refs of an install are applied first and together, which is why a run killed at that boundary is found with every branch written and no library directory made; the refs of a removal, which only delete, are applied last, so a removal killed at that boundary is found with a branch and nothing for it to name. See the [mutation journal](#mutation-journal). The lock is taken once before the network as well, to recover an unfinished journal and to refuse a run that could not write anyway; the fetch of the blobs runs outside it.

**A skill that cannot be installed** costs only itself: a run of several skills drops it with a `log` warning naming it and the reason, the rest of the run lands, and the `error` and `result` name every skill that failed and why. A run over several skills, of any command, names a skill in front of its reason as `<name>: <message>`, unless the message already starts with the name, `<name> ...` or `<name>'s ...`, which it then gives as it is. What is refused this way is a name the library or the account repo cannot hold, a directory the import commit cannot record, a skill directory with no regular file in it at all, an entry whose path would lay it out outside the skill's directory, a name a second skill of the same run also claims, a library directory that holds something else, a name taken by a fork or by another version, each exit code 6, and a file the source did not serve, exit code 5 with a hint to fetch the source again, as a listing of an incomplete source answers. Every refusal is decided before the run records a step for that skill, so a skill that fails leaves neither a branch nor a directory behind. The directory is refused on the rule the trailer reader keeps and before the version is read, as the name is: a subpath the reader would not read back as the directory it names is one carrying a C0 control character or `DEL`, which no trailer carries at all, and one opening or closing with whitespace, which a trailer hands back trimmed and so reads as another directory of that same source. A C1 control character is recorded and read back as it is. The directory is checked ahead of the entries under it, so a skill whose directory cannot be recorded is refused for that even when an entry below it would be refused too. The three coordinates agentx writes itself are held to the reader as well, on the whole message the commit would carry, and a version they fail is exit code 10 rather than a refusal: a canonical URL, an object id or a content hash that the reader does not take back is agentx's own bug and no fault of the version. Writing lineage anyway would install the skill with the lineage its import commit exists to provide already severed, silently: the listing would give it as `managed` with no `source`, no `subpath`, no `upstream_commit`, no `base_hash` and no `state`, or with a `subpath` naming a directory it did not come from, and no later update could put either right. The exit code is the one every failure agrees on and exit code 6 when they disagree, the way exit code 3 answers for a fetch of several sources; one skill that failed in a run that installed nothing answers exactly as installing that one skill would. Anything that is not about one skill: an unreachable source, an unusable account repo, a name `--name` or `--except` does not find, fails the whole run before it starts.

**After the install** the affected configurations are read again, a scan of the machine like any other, and the `library_skill` event reports the placements that rescan found in the configurations this install covered, rather than the ones it meant to make; `skill list` reports every placement of every library skill. That rescan, and the read of the library the report is built from, are made once for the whole run, whatever the number of skills it installed: reading the library content-hashes every directory it holds, work that grows with the library and not with the run, so a read per installed skill would cost a batch of n skills n passes over the whole library. The run emits the `source` event first, the one `source add` emits for a source the run added or fetched and the one `source skills` emits otherwise, so its `last_fetched` says how old the fetch the install read from is; then one `progress` per step per skill, `blobs`, `import` and `install`, then one for the `rescan`, then one `library_skill` event per skill it installed, in the order it installed them. `total` is `3 × <skills selected> + 1`, and a skill the run gives up on takes the steps it will no longer run out of it, so a run that ends emits a last event whose `current` is its `total`. The `result` of a run that installed everything it selected summarises what happened: `installed <name> from <url>[ under <subpath>] in <n> configurations` for one skill, `adopted` instead of `installed` when the library already held the version, `installed <name>, <name>, ... from <url> in <n> configurations` for several, with `, <n> already in the library` when some were adopted, what was made as a copy, adopted or skipped, then `, source fetched <last_fetched>`, and `; always available to universal clients: <ids, comma separated>` last, in the order of `universal`, when any universal client is detected; the list closes the line so that its commas cannot be read as those between the clauses. A run with a skill it could not install ends with `ok` `false` and the `error` message as its summary.

**Placing later.** `agentx skill place <name> [--to <configuration>]... [--copy] [--force]` places a skill the library already holds. It is the placement half of an install on its own: the same targets, every enabled configuration or exactly those `--to` names, and the same `copy_mode` write, so a placement made later is the placement the install would have made. It also puts back what `drift` names at the places it covers, see **Putting placements back** below. An id that names no detected configuration is exit code 5 with a hint listing the detected ones, whatever its enabled state, since naming one is asking for it. A name the library does not hold as a skill, other than a fork's, which is found by its branch (see **Putting a fork back** below), is exit code 5 with a hint to list the library; a directory of the library with no `SKILL.md` is no skill of the library either and is left where it is. For a universal client the library entry is already the placement and nothing is made, as during an install. The command emits one `library_skill` event with the placements the rescan found in the configurations it covered and every universal client in `universal`, whatever `--to` named, and its `result` is `placed <name> in <n> configurations`, with `, <n> placements as copy`, `, <n> placements adopted` and `, <n> placements skipped` when there were any, then `; discarded what <path>, ... held` after `--force` replaced a directory that differed, then `; always available to universal clients: <ids, comma separated>` as an install ends its own.

**Removal.** `agentx skill remove <name> [--from <configuration>|universal]... [--remote]` takes a skill out of the configurations `--from` names, or, with `--from universal` or no `--from`, off the machine: every placement, the library directory, the import branch, its candidate ref `refs/agentx/candidate/<name>`, its upstream-removed marker `refs/agentx/upstream-removed/<name>` and its `copy_mode` entries, as one journaled mutation. A fork comes off the machine with its worktree and its branch too, see **Removing a fork** below. A name the library does not hold as a skill is exit code 5, unless the account repo holds an import branch for it, `refs/heads/managed/<name>`, and no fork: that is a managed skill whose library directory was deleted, or left without its `SKILL.md`, outside agentx, which a listing warns about, see **Listing** below. Without `--from` or with `--from universal` its removal takes what is left of it off the machine, always, whatever the source holds by now: the import branch, deleted with the commit it was read at as its expected old value, its candidate ref, its upstream-removed marker, its `copy_mode` entries, and each placement by the rule every removal deletes by, a symlink naming the library directory, dangling now, or a recorded copy, anything else being left in place and named. A recorded copy is often the last of the skill on the machine, so it is compared with the base version the branch names, as git would record the two, since there is no library to compare it with. A branch whose commit carries no lineage agentx can read names no base version to compare a copy with, so every recorded copy the removal deletes counts as different. A copy that is anything else goes with the `warning:` `<id>'s copy of <name> was different from its base version; removing it deleted those changes (<path>)`, a `log` event at level `warn` with `--json`. A directory still at the library path is not the skill and is left too, with the `warning:` `<path> holds no skill and was left as it is; move it aside before installing <name> again`, `<path>` printed as every other warning prints a path. The branch and the library path are read again under the lock, and a branch that moved, or a library that came to hold the skill meanwhile, is exit code 6 with nothing removed. `--from <configuration>` naming such a skill is exit code 6, `the library holds no skill directory for <name>, so it cannot be removed from <id> alone`, with the hint `take what is left of it off the machine with 'agentx skill remove <name>'`: with no library entry there is no skill for one configuration to keep while another loses it. A removal that takes the skill off the machine, with no `--from` or with `--from universal`, of a skill with a merge pending (see **A merge pending** below) is exit code 4, whether or not its library directory is still there, and removes nothing: the merge holds the library directory and the import branch it would take away. Taking the skill out of one configuration leaves both, and is not refused.

**`--from universal`** names the removal by what it does to the universal clients: they read the library entry itself, so removing the skill from them is removing that entry, which takes it from every client. It is the removal without `--from` and runs as that removal does, the removal of a fork included, with the same output; everything said below of a removal without `--from` holds for it. Given more than once it is one request. Beside a configuration whose client is not universal it is exit code 1, as `--all` beside `--name` is: the whole removal already takes every placement, so a configuration named beside it can only mean the removal was expected to do less than it does. Whether a client is universal is read from the machine, so this is decided once the library is found to hold the name and every other `--from` value names a detected configuration, a name the library does not hold or an id no detected configuration has being exit code 5 first. Beside a universal client, whatever else `--from` names, it is exit code 6, the refusal of the next paragraph, and not exit code 1, whose hint to drop `--from universal` would only lead to that refusal. `universal` is reserved: no configuration has that id, the registry leaving out the skills CLI's universal pseudo-agent.

**What a removal may delete.** A placement is deleted only when it is a symlink that names the library directory, dangling or not, or a real directory this machine recorded in `copy_mode` for that configuration. Anything else at a placement path – a directory the user made by hand, a symlink of theirs pointing somewhere else however it resolves, a directory holding exactly the library's version that nothing recorded as a copy, a regular file – is left exactly as it is, is one `warning:` line on stderr naming it and saying what it is, and is counted in the `result` as `<n> placements left in place`. The line for a symlink of the user's names what it points at and ends `<id> still sees it`, unless its chain of links, followed one link at a time, goes through a path the same removal deletes: the library entry when the skill comes off the machine, or a placement the removal deletes. Such a link stays but no longer leads to the skill, and the line ends `it no longer leads to <name>` instead. A placement path holding nothing is neither deleted nor left: there was nothing there. Leaving a placement alone does not fail the run, the way a skipped placement does not fail an install. A copy the settings record is deleted as asked even when its content is no longer the library's version, which is a `warning:` line of its own, worded like the one for a copy a placement leaves unchanged, so that what went does not go unsaid: `warning: <id>'s copy of <name> was different from the library; removing it deleted those changes (<path>)`, `<path>` printed as every other warning prints a placement path. With `--json` it is one `log` event of level `warn` whose message is that line without `warning: `. The line says only that the copy was different from the library, never how it came to differ, since nothing records whether the user edited the copy, replaced it with something else or had a directory of their own adopted there. Content the removal displaces is retained beside the live path and dropped only once every path holds its new state and it still hashes to what the journal captured, so an edit made while the removal ran is kept, see the [mutation journal](#mutation-journal).

**A universal-library occurrence cannot be removed for one client.** For a universal client, `reads_library` in a snapshot, the library entry is the occurrence it is judged by, reached through a skills directory it reads, and taking it away takes the skill from every other client that sees it too; a link or copy it may still hold in its own skills directory, as Cursor holds `~/.cursor/skills/<name>` once Claude Code's skills directory is a symlink to the library, is not removed for it alone. `--from` naming a universal client is exit code 6, whatever its enabled state and whatever other configurations `--from` names beside it, `universal` among them: the whole request is refused before the lock is taken, before any journal is written and before anything is deleted, so a run cannot half do what it cannot do at all and leaves nothing for a later command to recover. The message is `<id> reads the library directly, so <name> cannot be removed from it alone`, or `<id>, <id> read the library directly, so <name> cannot be removed from them alone` for several, naming the universal clients `--from` named in id order. The hint is `take <name> off the machine with 'agentx skill remove <name> --from universal', which removes it from <ids, comma separated>`, the ids being every detected configuration whose client sees the skill through a path that removal deletes, sorted by id: the library entry for a universal client, and for any client a symlink naming the library directory, or a copy recorded for the configuration whose skills directory holds it, in any user skills directory the client reads, its own or another client's, as Cursor reads Claude Code's, or a link of the user's there whose chain of links leads through the library entry or through such a symlink or copy: the removal leaves that link, since agentx did not make it, but it no longer leads to the skill. A directory of the user's that the removal would leave in place takes the skill from no one and is not counted, and neither is a link that reaches the skill's files without going through a path the removal deletes, such as a link straight to the directory a library entry that is itself a link names; a universal client with such a directory of its own is counted all the same, since it loses the library skill, and the removal warns about the directory that stays. A fork is answered the same way, since `--from universal` removes a fork too. With `--from universal` or without `--from` the universal clients stop seeing the skill because the library entry goes, and each is reported as a placement of mode `library`.

**What else a removal without `--from` takes.** The library directory, whatever kind of entry it is, and, when the account repo holds them, `refs/heads/managed/<name>`, `refs/agentx/candidate/<name>` and `refs/agentx/upstream-removed/<name>`, each deleted with the value it holds now, so a branch something else moved is refused rather than dropped. Those deletions are the last steps of the mutation, after the library directory and every placement, so a removal that cannot finish still has the skill's lineage to answer with. A name the account repo holds as a fork, `refs/heads/skills/<name>`, is removed as **Removing a fork** below says, its placements still taken away one configuration at a time with `--from <configuration>` when asked, and a name that becomes a fork between the read before the lock and the one under it is exit code 6 with nothing removed. The configurations a removal covers are the detected ones; a configuration that is no longer detected keeps whatever it holds.

**After a removal or a placement** the affected configurations are read again, a scan of the machine like any other, as after an install. `skill place` and `skill remove --from <configuration>` emit one `library_skill` event carrying the placements that rescan found in the configurations the command covered, which for a removal is what is left there: a client that reads another client's skills directory, as Cursor reads Claude Code's, may still see the skill by a path this command did not touch, and the event says so. A removal that took the skill off the machine emits no `library_skill` event, the library holding nothing to report, and fails with exit code 10 if the library still holds the name. With no event to correct it, that removal reports on its own claim instead: every configuration it covered that the rescan still finds a skill of that name in is one `warning:` line on stderr naming the configuration and the path it reads it at, since a client that reads the library loses only the library entry and a directory of its own under that name is one the removal never looks at. A placement the run already reported as left in place is not named a second time. Neither command emits `progress`: each does one thing once. The `result` of a removal is `removed <name> from the library, <n> placements and its import branch` when the skill left the machine and had one, `removed <name>, which the library no longer held: its import branch and <n> placements` when the library held no directory for it, `removed <name> from the library and <n> placements` when it had none, and `removed <name> from <the configurations it named, comma separated>: <n> placements` for a removal with `--from <configuration>`; `, <n> placements left in place` ends the line when any were. That count includes the library-mode placements of the clients that read the library, which lose the skill with the library entry.

**Files that do not count.** A skill directory is a git work tree, and a managed skill's state, its diff, an update and the copy step judge it as git judges one, over a throwaway index loaded from the base version (see [Git](#git)): git ignores what the skill's own `.gitignore` files name, with git's patterns, negation, directory patterns and precedence, what the user's global ignore file names, and, while `ignore_system_files` is on, the files an operating system or an editor leaves on its own, see [Machine settings](#machine-settings); and it applies the skill's own `.gitattributes`, a line-ending conversion included, with `core.attributesFile` kept at the null device so the user's own attributes file never applies. A file the base version holds always counts, whatever an ignore rule says of it, since the index already tracks it. A file git ignores never makes a skill `modified`, is never in its diff, and is kept by an update.

**Comparing with the base.** `agentx skill diff <name>` shows how the library directory of a managed skill differs from its base version, the tree of the import commit its branch points at, as one unified diff per file, which git writes: its edits not yet published, since a publish to its source makes what it published the base version (see **Publishing a skill of a shared source**). The directory is read in process as git would record it, as `state` reads it. One that `state` reads as `current` gets no `diff` event and a `result` of `<name> matches its base version at <short upstream commit>`, which is said of that directory and of no other. It costs no git beyond the lineage when its tree id already tells, as for `state`. Otherwise git reads the directory as `state` does, `read-tree`, `add -A` and `write-tree` over a throwaway index loaded from the base version, and compares the base version with that index, `git diff --cached -z --no-renames --name-status` for the status of every file and `git diff --cached -p --no-renames --no-color` for the diffs, so a file git ignores is never in the diff and a file a `.gitattributes` of the skill converts is compared as git converts it. Nothing is written to the library. The command emits one `diff` event per file, sorted by path, and a `result` of `<name> differs from its base version at <short upstream commit> in <n> files`. Every path is relative to the skill's directory: the import tree holds the skill under the upstream's own directory name, which is taken off, so a skill whose frontmatter name is not its upstream directory's, and one at the root of its repository, compare the same way. Renames are not looked for, a file moved being one deleted and one added, which is what happened to the paths an agent reads. What git cannot record, a `.git` component or a named pipe, is left out with one `warning:` per path naming it, and never reads as matching, as it never reads as `current`: the `result` then ends `, and <n> paths git cannot record` (`1 path`), or is `<name> differs from its base version at <short upstream commit> only in <n> paths git cannot record` when nothing else differs, a skill `skill list` reports as `modified`, and an update of it refuses. A name the library does not hold as a skill is exit code 5; an unmanaged skill and a managed skill whose import branch carries no lineage agentx can read are exit code 6, neither having a base version this command reads, and so is `--commit` for any skill that is not one of your own, `<name> is not a skill of the account remote, so it has no commit of its own to compare with` for a skill of a shared source or `<name> is not a skill of the account remote, so it has no commit to compare with` for an unmanaged one. `--update` and `--commit` together are exit code 1. A fork is compared with its own commits instead, see **Comparing a fork** below. Adoption offers no diff of its own: `skill diff` compares an adopted skill as it compares any other. With `--update` the command compares the base version with the update a check pinned instead, see **Checking for updates** below. Without `--json` the command prints the line its `result` holds, `<name> matches its base version at <short upstream commit>`, or `<name> differs from its base version at <short upstream commit> in <n> files`, or either line about the paths git cannot record, followed by every diff, printed as [Streams](#streams) prescribes for a diff, the name sanitised.

**Putting placements back.** `skill place` judges every place it covers exactly as `drift` judges it, at each covered configuration's own place, a path two configurations share, or name differently because one's skills directory is a symlink to the other's, being one place, judged and written once. It judges an unmanaged skill the same way, though the listing reports no `drift` for one. What is written depends on what the place holds:

- **Nothing**, `missing`: the placement is made, a copy where `copy_mode` records one for a configuration of that place or `--copy` asks for one, and a symlink naming the library directory everywhere else.
- **The symlink naming the library directory where a copy belongs**, `displaced` where `copy_mode` records one: a symlink holds nothing, so it is replaced by a copy of the library directory without a flag.
- **A real directory where agentx keeps a symlink**, `displaced`: the directory and the library directory are read in process as git would record them, every file counting and nothing ignored, or byte for byte where it or the library directory holds something git cannot record, a `.git` component or a named pipe. One that holds exactly what the library directory holds is adopted and reported as adopted: it is replaced by the placement, a `remove`, which retains it until the mutation is verified, and a `link`, or a `publish` of a copy with `--copy`. Replacing a directory holding anything else would discard what it holds, so without `--force` the command is exit code 6 and changes nothing at all, the other places included: the message names every such directory, `<path> is a directory whose content differs from the library's <name>, so nothing was placed`, or `<path>, <path> are directories whose content differs from the library's <name>, so nothing was placed` naming several, and the hint names `--force`, what it deletes and how to keep the directory instead, `to replace it with the library's version and delete what it holds, run 'agentx skill place <name> --force'; to keep it, move it elsewhere first`, with `them` and `they hold` for several. `--force` replaces every such directory the run covers with the placement, through a `remove` and a `link`, or a `publish` of a copy with `--copy`, and what it held is discarded once the mutation is verified. The library directory is never changed: content worth keeping is moved out of the client's skills directory by hand before the run. `--force` changes nothing where no displaced directory differs from the library.
- **Anything else**: a symlink of the user's to anywhere else, a copy whose content is not the library's and anything else at the place are no drift, and are left as they are, each named in a warning and counted as skipped, as an install leaves them (see **Placements**), with or without a flag. A configuration the run does not cover, a universal client and a real directory that is the library directory itself, which a library entry made a symlink to it leaves at a configuration's own place, are never touched.

**Putting a fork back.** For a fork, `skill place <name>` first puts back what the fork needs on this machine, as serve does when it starts (see **Reconciliation** under [Serve](#serve)), then places it as an install places it, a directory that differs at a place being left in place with a warning. It finds the fork by its branch, whatever its library entry is. A worktree whose pointers no longer meet is repaired with `git worktree repair <path>`, outside any journal, since the repair is git's own and safe to repeat. A worktree that is gone, or was never checked out on this machine, is added again from the branch, its skill directory laid out from the tip byte for byte as the account repo holds it, what the tip holds beside the skill directory, such as a `.gitignore` committed with git, checked out with the worktree, so the worktree comes back clean and goes on ignoring what it ignored, and the library symlink written, a dangling one removed first; a skill directory missing from a worktree on its branch is laid out the same way, together with what the tip holds beside it when the worktree holds nothing but its `.git` file, and a missing or dangling library symlink is written. It is one journal with the placements, under the lock: `ref` holding the branch at the tip the content is laid out from, `worktree`, `publish`, `remove` of a dangling symlink, `link`, then the placements and the settings. Something of the user's in the way of the fork, an adopt candidate, is exit code 6 before anything changes: `<path> is in the way of <name>'s <worktree|library symlink>, so nothing was placed`, with the hint `run 'agentx skill place <name> --force' to adopt it: its content becomes the skill's unpublished edits`, or, for a symlink of the user's at the library entry, `run 'agentx skill place <name> --force' to replace it with the fork's library symlink`. `--force` adopts it and deletes nothing. A directory at the worktree that git does not register, holding the fork's skill directory and at most a `.git` file whose registration is gone, keeps its content in place: the journal moves the skill directory to a hidden directory of the worktrees directory, `.agentx-adopting-<id>-<n>`, which no sweep removes, adds the worktree, which git refuses into a directory that holds anything, and moves it back (`move`, `worktree`, `move`, then the library symlink when it is needed). A directory at the library entry is moved into the worktree as the fork's skill directory (`worktree`, `remove` of a skill directory there that holds the branch tip, retained until the journal completes, `move`, `link`); that is refused, exit code 6, when the directory is itself a Git repository, when the library and agentx home are on different file systems, when the worktree's skill directory has edits of its own, which only publishing or discarding them settles, and when it holds files git ignores or cannot record, such as a local `.env`, each named, since they would go with it. A worktree where a git command the user ran stopped part way, or whose index a running git holds, is refused as a publish refuses it (see **Publishing** below) whenever placing would add it again or lay a skill directory out in it, since that aligns its index with the branch. A symlink of the user's at the library entry holds nothing and is replaced. Adopted content reads as the fork's unpublished edits, which `skill publish` records. A worktree holding anything else beside its skill directory, what the fork's branch holds there included, since git adds a worktree only into an empty directory and a journal moves directories alone, a skill directory that is itself a Git repository, or a `.git` that names a registration git could not repair, or the registration of another worktree that is still there, as copying a worktree leaves it, is refused even with `--force`, naming what is in it, `<path> holds <path>, ... beside <name>'s skill directory <dir>, so it cannot be adopted`, with the hint `move them out of it, then run 'agentx skill place <name> --force' again; what the fork's branch holds beside its skill directory is checked out from the branch`: the adoption checks the branch's own files out again, so one moved out comes back as the branch holds it. So is a directory at the library entry while a directory at the worktree is in the way too. A worktree on another branch than the fork's, its skill directory gone, is exit code 6, `<name>'s worktree <path> is not on its branch skills/<name>`, with the hint `run 'git -C <path> switch skills/<name>' to put it back on its branch`, since laying the skill directory out there would commit it to another branch. A fork whose branch moved after `skill place` read it is exit code 6, `<name> changed while it was being placed, so nothing was changed`, with the hint `run 'agentx skill place <name>' again`, its flags included. Files `ignore_system_files` ignores while it is on, a `.DS_Store` say, are no user's: they go with a skill directory a library directory replaces and refuse nothing.
