Then what the library path holds decides, before anything is written: nothing, or a symlink into the worktrees directory that leads nowhere, installs the skill; a managed skill of the same name whose base version has the source and the subpath the remote tip's base version records, and whose library directory holds that base version as `state` judges it, under Git's ignore rules, is superseded; any other directory, a managed skill whose library directory has edits, an unmanaged one or a managed skill of another upstream, is exit code 6, `the library already holds <path>, so <name> was not installed`, with the hint `move it aside, or run 'agentx skill remove <name>', then run 'agentx skill add --name <name>' again`, since installing would lose what it holds; a symlink of any other kind is exit code 6, `the library already holds <path>, a symlink, so <name> was not installed`, with the hint to remove the link; anything else is exit code 6, `<path> is neither a directory nor a symlink`. A managed skill of the name whose library directory is gone is exit code 6, its hint naming `agentx skill remove <name>`. A remote tip whose skill directory holds no `SKILL.md` file is exit code 5 with nothing changed. All of these refusals come before anything is written. When the remote tip records a base version whose source the settings hold no entry for, the source is added by the steps `agentx source add` takes, with its own `source` event and line, so that update checks cover the skill on this machine and **The recorded base** can prove one upstream version newer than another; a source that cannot be fetched does not stop the install: a warning says `<name>'s upstream <url> could not be fetched, so it was not added: <reason>; run 'agentx source add <url>' to receive its updates`, or, when a stop of the run kept the source out, `<name>'s upstream <url> was not added because the run was stopped; run 'agentx source add <url>' to receive its updates`. Under the lock every input is read again, the branches, the worktree path and the library path, and anything that moved is exit code 6 with nothing changed. One journal then holds the install: `ref` creating `skills/<name>` at the remote tip, with an empty expected old value; `worktree`, which adds the worktree with nothing checked out but what the tip holds beside the skill directory and resets its index; the skill directory, published from the tip byte for byte as the account repo holds it, staged in the worktrees directory, the files git ignores in a superseded copy carried in as every replacement carries them; `remove` of what the library path held, a superseded directory retained until the journal completes or a symlink that led nowhere; `link` of the library path to the skill directory; then, for a skill new to this machine, the placements of an install, `placementTargets` of `--to` with `--copy`, and the settings; or, when the skill takes the place of a managed skill, the placements and its `copy_mode` as they are, since they name the library path, so a configuration the managed skill was removed from stays without it, and last, deleted, its import branch, its update candidate and its upstream-removed marker. `--to` or `--copy` given then places nothing and says so in a warning. No commit is written and no content is copied outside git. Just before the journal is written, `branch.skills/<name>.remote=src-<id>`, the account remote's remote, and `branch.skills/<name>.merge=refs/heads/skills/<name>` are written with `git config`, so `git status` in the worktree compares it with the remote-tracking branch, an install a later command finishes included; agentx itself never reads them, a section left for a branch the journal never creates is harmless, and a failure is a warning. Each skill installed emits its `library_skill`; without `--json` it prints `✓ installed <name> from the account remote at <short commit>: <n> placements` with the placement rows of an install, or, superseding a managed skill, `✓ installed <name> from the account remote at <short commit>; it replaces the managed skill wherever it was`. The `result` of a run of one skill is `installed <name> from the account remote in <n> configurations`, or, when it takes the place of a managed skill, `installed <name> from the account remote; it replaces the managed skill wherever it was`; of a run of several, `installed <k> of <n> skills from the account remote: <name>, ...`.

**The account step.** The account step of `agentx skill update` (see **Updating a fork** under [Skills](#skills)) brings a fork up to date with its branch on the account remote, as the run's one fetch left it. `agentx skill publish` never takes it, see **Publishing** under [Skills](#skills). A fork with a merge pending never takes it: the update completes the merge or reports it again, as **A fork's merge pending** under [Skills](#skills) describes. A fork the remote holds no branch of, or whose branch holds the remote tip already, the same commit or a descendant of it, as one `git merge-base <tip> <remote tip>` naming the remote tip says, takes nothing in, whatever its worktree holds, and nothing is reported of it. A remote branch that is another fork is refused, see above. A remote branch that shares no commit with the fork is exit code 6. Then the worktree's health is checked as a publish checks it, and a repository nested in its skill directory that no ignore rule covers, or anything else git cannot record that no rule covers, refuses the fork, exit code 6, all before anything is written. Then the fork's unpublished edits are recorded on its branch, see **Unpublished edits** under [Skills](#skills): the remote tip does not descend from that commit, so the fork is merged rather than fast-forwarded past them, and they stay unpublished. A fork whose tip the remote tip descends from is fast-forwarded to it, with no commit written. Any other is merged by a plain Git merge on the ancestry the two histories share: one `merge-tree --write-tree` of the local tip and the remote tip with git's own merge base, the configuration and the sizing of conflict markers of **Updating an edited skill**. A clean merge is committed outside the lock by the fork commit writer (see **Fork commits** under [Git](#git)), with the local tip and the remote tip as parents, the subject `<name>: merge the account remote (<machine label>)` and the trailers `Agentx-Base`, by the rule of **The recorded base** below, and `Agentx-Machine`. Under the lock the fork's branch and its remote-tracking branch are read again in one `for-each-ref`: a branch that no longer holds the tip is exit code 6, `<name> changed while it was being updated, so nothing was changed`, and a remote-tracking branch that no longer holds the remote tip, as another run's fetch moves it, is exit code 6, `the account remote's skills/<name> moved while <name> was being updated, so nothing was changed`, each with the hint to run the update again; a merge pending since is exit code 4; and the worktree's health and the skill directory are judged again, as for an update. The fork then moves in a journal of its own, the journal of **Updating a fork** under [Skills](#skills): the branch from the tip to the new tip, the tip as its expected old value, so that a commit made in the worktree meanwhile abandons the journal, exit code 6, with nothing changed; the skill directory retained and laid out from the new tip, the files git ignores carried in; the worktree's index reset; the copies that held the tip refreshed; and, last, the candidate ref deleted when it names the import the new tip records as its base, as it does once another machine merged the same update, or when the new tip moves the fork's base to another import and the source history does not prove the candidate newer than that import, by the test of **The recorded base** below: a candidate the last check pinned against the old base, older than the version another machine took, would otherwise merge the older version back over the newer one. The next update check pins an update against the new base. A merge pending that an update completes deletes the candidate by the same rule. A merge that conflicts changes nothing: it is left pending as an update's is, in `<agentx home>/merges/<name>`, detached at the tip, with `ORIG_HEAD` the tip, `MERGE_HEAD` the remote tip and `MERGE_MSG` the message of the commit that completes it, the subject above with the base the rule below chooses. The `conflict` event's `base` is the merge base of the two tips, `mine` the tip and `theirs` the remote tip; the line is `<name> conflicts with the account remote in <n> files`, and that fork is exit code 4, `<name> conflicts with the account remote in <n> files, so the merge is pending and the fork's worktree and branch were left as they are`, with the hint of **Conflicts** naming `agentx skill update <name>`. Once it is resolved with git in the checkout, `agentx skill update <name>` completes it, and `agentx skill update <name> --abort` gives it up. A fork whose account step took something in, conflicted or was refused gets one `pull` event and, for a fast-forward or a merge, the line `✓ pulled <name>: fast-forward to <short commit>` or `✓ pulled <name>: merged the account remote, committed as <short commit>`, then the update's own events, as **Updating a fork** describes.

**The recorded base.** The commit that merges two histories of one fork records in `Agentx-Base` which import is the fork's base afterwards, since its next update from upstream merges with that import as its base; a fork with no upstream has none and records none. When both sides name the same base, that is it. Every machine writes the same import commit for an upstream version, so two machines that took the same version and edited nothing since merge clean. Until they have synced once, though, the import and the commit they last shared are two merge bases with no common ancestor, and git merges over a virtual base in which every line where those two differ conflicts: each line the version changed, and each line the fork had changed from upstream before it took the version. A commit on either machine that edits such a line, or a line next to one, conflicts on the other's update, even when the other edited nothing. Once either machine has merged the other's commits, that merge is their one base. When they name two versions of the same source and subpath, the newer one is the base when the source history the account repo has fetched proves it: one `rev-list --missing=print --no-walk` of the two `Agentx-Upstream-Commit` values finds both in the account repo without reaching any remote, and `merge-base --is-ancestor <local upstream commit> <remote upstream commit>` holding makes the remote's the base. Anything else keeps the local side's: a version whose source history the account repo never fetched, a source that was force pushed, two versions on diverged branches of the source, or bases of different sources or subpaths. Neither commit time nor the order versions were imported in is ever read. A base kept that way costs at most a conflict on the next update from upstream; no content is lost over it, since the merge itself is git's. The same rule decides the base of a completed merge merged again with commits made while it was pending, when those commits brought the fork a base of their own.

**Publishing.** `agentx skill publish` records the edits of your own skills and pushes their branches here, and never takes in what another machine published: see **Publishing** under [Skills](#skills).

## Export and import

`agentx export <file>` writes this machine's settings and one lineage record per branch of the account repo to `<file>` as one JSON document. `agentx import <file>` restores the settings of such a document, after confirming, and lists what the account repo of this machine can restore of the skills it records. `export` reads and writes that one file; `import` writes the settings file and nothing else. Nothing reads an export on its own: no command looks for one, there is no path a document is expected at, and an export left in agentx home is a file like any other.

**The document** is one JSON object, indented with two spaces and ending in a newline:

```json
{
  "schema_version": 1,
  "machine": { "id": "0123456789abcdef0123456789abcdef", "label": "my-laptop" },
  "settings": { "schema_version": 1, "label": "my-laptop", "...": "the machine settings file" },
  "skills": [
    {
      "name": "pdf",
      "kind": "managed",
      "commit": "<the commit refs/heads/managed/pdf points at>",
      "source": "https://github.com/example/skills",
      "subpath": "skills/pdf",
      "upstream_commit": "<the commit the version was read from>",
      "base_hash": "<the content hash of that version>",
      "placed": true
    }
  ]
}
```

| Field | Meaning |
|---|---|
| `schema_version` | the document's own version, currently `1`. It is neither the settings schema version inside it nor the output schema version an event carries; a document of another version is refused |
| `machine.id` | the [machine id](#machine-identity) of the machine that exported |
| `machine.label` | its label as a command reports it: the setting, or the hostname while it is not set |
| `settings` | the [machine settings](#machine-settings) exactly as the settings file holds them, the label not filled in from the hostname, so that importing them does not give another machine this one's hostname |
| `skills` | one record per branch of the account repo, over `refs/heads/managed/` and `refs/heads/skills/`, sorted by name; its fields are those of the `import_skill` event above without `state` and `local_commit`, each as `skill list` reports it in the skill's `library_skill`. Every record is of kind `managed`. A skill of a shared source names that source and its subpath; one of your own, a branch under `refs/heads/skills/`, names the account remote as its `source`, with no subpath, or no source at all when no account remote is set, and a forked one its `upstream` and `upstream_subpath`. `upstream_commit` and `base_hash` are those of the version the skill is based on, a forked skill's being the import commit its history names; a skill with no upstream, as one `skill new` made, has none |

**What it never holds.** No snapshot, no tree, no blob, no file of any skill, and not the frontmatter name or description either: a record names a version, it never carries one. What restores content is the account repo, which holds it, and an install from the source, which fetches it, which is exactly why an import only lists. Nothing about the run reaches the document, no date and no order of its own, so two exports of an unchanged machine are byte-identical.

**Writing it.** `agentx export` reads the settings, then the branches in one `for-each-ref` over both namespaces as [`skill list`](#skills) does, and, only when there is a branch at all, runs a scan of the machine to decide `placed`; that boolean is all of the scan that reaches the document. The file is created readable by its owner alone, since it holds the machine settings, which never leave the machine except this way. A path that is already there is exit code 6 with a hint naming `--force`, which overwrites it; a path that names a directory is exit code 6 with a hint naming the path of a file, since `--force` cannot overwrite one; a path whose directory does not exist is exit code 5; any other failure to write is exit code 10. A branch of either namespace whose name the library cannot hold as a directory names no skill and is left out of the document, with one `warning:` line naming the ref: agentx makes no such branch, but the namespaces are shared with the account remote, and writing one into the document would write a record the reader below refuses, costing that reader the whole document over a branch that names no skill. A branch of the managed namespace whose commit carries no lineage trailers names no source, and a record with no source reads as one of your own skills, so it too is left out, with one `warning:` line naming the ref. An account repo that does not exist is not created for a listing: the document then lists no skill.

**Reading one.** `agentx import` reads the document whole and refuses anything it cannot restore before it takes the lock, so that a document agentx will not accept costs the settings on disk nothing at all. A file that is not there is exit code 5; any other failure to read it, a directory or an unreadable file, is exit code 6, and so is a file larger than 8 MiB, which is more than a machine's settings and lineage records come to and bounds every field of a document at once. Exit code 6, naming the file and the reason, is also what is given to a document that is not JSON, that is truncated, that carries anything after its one object, that carries no `schema_version` or one this agentx does not read, or that carries a field this agentx does not know – in the document or in its settings, since a field that was dropped silently would be a setting lost on restore. The settings it holds must be settings agentx would write itself, in every field and not only the ones a later command would notice: an import is the one route by which a string somebody else chose reaches the settings file, so a field nothing else in the CLI writes has this gate and no other in front of it. Their `schema_version` is the one this CLI writes; the label is one the label rule above accepts; `disabled_configurations` holds configuration ids, sorted, none of them twice; `copy_mode` maps skill names to configuration ids and is spelled as agentx spells it, which is an object sorted by key, each skill once and none of them with no configuration at all; and each source is one entry, the list sorted by `url` and no source in it twice, with `pin` and `default_branch` refs git accepts – the same check `source add <url>#<ref>` makes, since an import is equally a command that hands a ref of someone else's choosing to git – `last_fetched` and `access_checked` RFC 3339 in UTC, `account` on one entry at most and never on one with a `pin`, `<url> is pinned to <pin>, and the account remote is a whole repository`, `access` absent, `writable` or `read-only`, and no shared source naming the repository of the account remote, every host read as itself since an import reads no SSH configuration. Both `url` and `alias` are URLs of a source and are held to the same rule: every source is stored by its canonical URL alone, a URL carrying a user or a token included, which reaches the settings by no route at all and may not reach them by this one, `alias` being the only field of the settings that no other command writes and so the only one an import can be the first to fill. Its records must be records a branch could carry: a name the library can hold as a directory and a branch can carry, no name twice, the kind `managed` (any other kind is refused, `<file> lists a skill agentx cannot read: the kind of <name> is not managed`), a commit that is an object id, and lineage the trailer reader accepts, which is the reader of [the import commit](#skills) run over the message the version's four fields would make. A record whose `source` is the account remote of the document's settings, or that has no `source`, is one of your own skills and belongs under `refs/heads/skills/`: it carries no `subpath`, and its version, if any, is its `upstream`, `upstream_subpath`, `upstream_commit` and `base_hash`. Every other record is a skill of a shared source and belongs under `refs/heads/managed/`: it carries no `upstream` or `upstream_subpath`, and its version is its `source`, `subpath`, `upstream_commit` and `base_hash`.

**Confirming.** An import replaces the machine settings, which are what only this machine decides, so it is never the default answer. `--yes` is how a caller says yes in advance. Without it, and only when the run is not in JSON mode and both stdin and stdout are terminals, the command asks on stdout, `Replace the settings in <settings path> with those of <label> from <file>? [y/N] `, and reads one line from stdin: `y` or `yes`, in any case and with any surrounding space, is a yes, and everything else is exit code 6 with the settings unchanged, an empty line and a stream that ends without one included. Where it cannot ask – in JSON mode, or with either stream not a terminal, which is every script and the desktop app – it refuses at once, exit code 6 with a hint naming `--yes`, rather than assuming the answer or waiting for input that will never come.

**What an import writes.** The settings file, whole, through the [mutation journal](#mutation-journal) as every settings write is, which bumps `version` like any mutation. Nothing else: no account repo is created, nothing is fetched, nothing is installed, nothing is placed and no library directory is written. The settings written are the document's, written as agentx writes settings, so a settings file agentx wrote round trips byte for byte, except the access fields, which an import resets: `access` and `access_checked` say what the exporting machine's credentials let it do at a source, which is no fact about this one, so they are left out of every source written and this machine checks again when it adds or fetches the source. They replace what this machine had, sources, disabled configurations and copy modes and all.

**The events.** With `--json` an import emits the `settings` event with the settings it wrote, then one `import_skill` per record of the document in the order the document holds them, then `result`.

**The listing.** Every record of the document is compared with what this machine's account repo holds under that name, read exactly as `skill list` reads it and empty when there is no account repo. A record is `present` when the repo holds that name, in the namespace the record belongs in (see `import_skill`), at that commit; `missing` when the repo holds no branch of that name; and `different` when it holds one at another commit or in the other namespace, and what it holds is reported beside it. A record of one of your own skills (its `source` the account remote of the document's settings, or absent) whose name the repo holds no `skills/<name>` branch of is compared with what the [account remote](#account-remote) held of it at the last fetch, its remote-tracking branch, read in one more `for-each-ref`: `present` at that commit, `different` at another. So a fresh machine that set the account remote, which `agentx source add <url> --account` creates the account repo for and fetches, holds every skill of yours published there. Nothing acts on the comparison: it is what tells the user which skills their account repo brings back on its own and which they have to install again. An account repo git cannot read is exit code 8 for either command, and for an import before anything is written: a machine whose repo is unusable is told so rather than given half an answer.

The `result` of an export is `exported the settings and <n> lineage records to <file>`; of an import, `imported the settings from <file>: <n> of <n> skills present, <n> missing, <n> at a different version`, or `imported the settings from <file>: the export lists no skills`.

Without `--json`: `export` prints `✓ exported the settings and <n> lineage records to <file>` and one indented line saying that the export holds no skill content; `import` prints `✓ imported the settings of <label> from <file>`, then `<n> skills in the export: <n> present, <n> missing, <n> at a different version` and one indented row per record, `<name>  <kind>  <state>  <source>`, the source written as `skill list` writes it, the account remote being the one the document's settings set and a record of your own that leaves its `source` out being published to it, or `No skills in the export.` when it lists none, then one indented line saying that only the settings were written, that each source is to be added again and that a missing skill is then installed with `agentx skill add <source>`, and under it one line per source of the settings, in their order and indented four spaces, `agentx source add <url>[#<pin>]`, or `agentx source add <url> --account` for the account remote, which names no pin since the account remote is a whole repository: an import restores the settings entry of a source and nothing of the source itself, so on the machine an import just made, a source has to be added again before a skill can be installed from it, and the line names the pin the settings hold because `source add` writes the pin its argument names, so the bare URL would unpin the source. These lines follow `No skills in the export.` too: a machine that added sources and installed nothing still has sources to add again. Each argument is printed as it is when it is made of letters, digits and `._/:@%+=,#-` alone and does not start with `#`, and otherwise in single quotes, each `'` in it written `'\''`, so that a POSIX shell reads it back as that one argument: a URL and a pin agentx accepts may still hold `$`, `&`, `;` or a quote, and pasted bare such a line would run a command the document chose. Settings that hold no source have none to add again: the indented line then names `agentx skill add <source>` alone, and nothing follows `No skills in the export.` Every value a document supplies is sanitised before it is printed, and before it is quoted, as [Streams](#streams) prescribes for text agentx did not write.

## Serve

`agentx serve --json` is the long-running child the desktop app holds open. It scans once on start, emits that snapshot, then rescans when the machine changes and emits the snapshot again only when the inventory changed. It runs until its stdin closes or its context is cancelled, then emits `result` and exits 0. Without `--json` it prints one line per event: `snapshot <counter>: <n> configurations, <n> skills`, `refresh <request_id>: ok, snapshot <counter>`, `refresh <request_id>: failed: <error>`, `search <request_id>: <n> results` (`1 result`), `update <name>: <short upstream commit> -> <short candidate upstream commit>, <n> files` (`1 file`), and for an update another machine published `update <name>: <short commit here> -> <short commit on the account remote>, <n> files`, or `reconcile <name>: <outcome>`, for every outcome but `restored`, the name sanitised.

One serve child per agentx home: on start it takes an exclusive advisory `flock` on `serve.lock` in agentx home and holds it until it exits. A second `agentx serve` for the same home, `--once` included, exits at once with code 6 and a hint naming `serve.lock`. This lock is distinct from `lock`: serve never blocks a mutating command.

Reconciliation: before its initial scan, a long-running serve matches the branches of the account repo against what is on disk, so that agentx installed again over an existing agentx home, or a fork's worktree deleted by mistake, comes back as it was, and emits one `reconcile` event per skill name, sorted by name, before the first `snapshot`. It holds the exclusive lock for it, waiting for it as the update check does, and does, in order: the recovery and pruning every mutation starts with, which removes a pending merge's checkout that is not registered and a registration with no checkout, so a pending merge is never repaired as a fork, nor one whose pointers moving agentx home broke; `worktree prune` when `worktree list --porcelain` shows a registration whose directory is gone, which every locked worktree, a fork's or a pending merge's, survives; `worktree repair` with the path of every fork's worktree whose pointers no longer meet and whose registration git can find, the one its `.git` file names or, when agentx home moved as a whole, the one of the same name, but never a directory under `worktrees/` that is no fork's worktree, nor a copy of a worktree whose registration names that worktree, which names it back, since the repair would hand the copy that worktree's registration; then the classification, from the filesystem and the lineage alone but for a managed skill's directory, which is compared with its base version as `state` compares it, so an ignored file never makes it `modified`; and one journal that repairs every fork whose worktree is missing, as `skill place` does (see **Putting a fork back** under [Skills](#skills)), with no placement touched, since a placement leads to the library entry the repair puts back. Nothing else changes: an adopt candidate is reported and left as it is. `version` is rewritten only when something was repaired. A fork whose worktree is registered and where a git command the user ran stopped part way, or whose index a running git holds, is not repaired when that would align its index, and is reported `worktree missing`. A repair that fails is a `log` warning prefixed `reconcile: `, its forks reported `worktree missing`, and serve goes on; so it does when reconciliation cannot run at all, which is a warning too.

`reconcile`:

| Field | Type | Meaning |
|---|---|---|
| `name` | string | the skill name: a branch's, a library directory's, or that of a fork branch the account remote holds |
| `kind` | string | `managed` for a skill with a branch, a fork branch of your own included, here or on the account remote, and `unmanaged` for a library directory no branch names |
| `outcome` | string | `restored`: a managed skill whose library directory holds its base version, or a fork whose library symlink leads into its registered worktree; `repaired`: a fork whose worktree, the skill directory in it, the worktree's pointers or its library symlink were missing or broken and were put back from its branch; `worktree missing`: a fork placed on this machine, as git's registration of its worktree, its worktree directory or its library symlink into the worktrees directory says, whose worktree, skill directory or library symlink is gone, or whose worktree's pointers no longer meet, and was not put back, an outcome of its own and never drift's `missing`, which is a configuration's place with nothing at it; `adopt candidate`: a fork with something of the user's in the way, a directory at its worktree that git does not register, or a directory or a symlink of the user's at its library entry; `modified`: a managed skill whose library directory differs from its base version; `installable`: a managed skill whose library directory is gone, a fork with no worktree, registration or library symlink on this machine, and a fork only the account remote's branches, `refs/remotes/src-<id>/skills/<name>`, hold; `unmanaged`: a skill directory of the library no branch names |
| `path` | string | what is in the way of an adopt candidate, and the worktree of a fork whose worktree is missing; absent otherwise |

Snapshots: every serve process has a fresh `instance_id` (or `AGENTX_INSTANCE_ID`). The initial scan always emits a `snapshot` with `scan_counter` `1`, even when an earlier process saw identical content. Each later scan is compared with the last emitted snapshot on its serialised bytes with `instance_id` and `scan_counter` excluded; an identical snapshot emits nothing, a different one increments the counter and is emitted whole. The desktop applies snapshots only from its active child and only with increasing counters, as [snapshot ordering](snapshot-ordering.md) prescribes.

Drift: serve keeps the `library` of the last snapshot it emitted and, after every later snapshot it emits, never after the initial one, emits one `drift` event per library skill in both whose `state` or `drift` changed, in the library's order, after that snapshot and before any `refresh_complete`. A skill that arrived in the library or left it is no transition, the snapshot saying so itself: a managed skill whose library directory went is named in its `warnings`. A snapshot whose library could not be listed, the account repo being one git cannot read, carries no library entries and a warning saying so, so every skill leaves the library and comes back across it: no transition is emitted across it, and the comparison starts again from the next snapshot that lists the library. An edit made in an editor reaches serve through the change signals below, within their debounce, as every other change does, and so does a commit made with git in a fork's worktree; serve never commits an edit itself: nothing watches a skill for drift on its own and nothing rescans on a timer. Serve keeps two timers: the update check's, below, which also refreshes every source, and an hourly one that maintains the account repo once a day (see **Maintenance**). Serve never pushes: every push is an explicit command. None rescans: what the update check records reaches the snapshot and the source index through the rescan its write of `version` sets off, as every mutation's does, and maintenance changes nothing a snapshot shows. Saving a file git ignores, a `.DS_Store` or an editor's swap file, changes no `state` and brings no `drift` event. Serve keeps git's verdict on a skill whose tree id does not tell until that tree id, its base version, the tip of a fork's branch or `ignore_system_files` changes, so a rescan that finds nothing changed runs no git over a skill directory; a change to the user's global ignore file alone is seen by the next serve child. The event is a notification: the snapshot before it carries the skill as it now is, and is what the desktop applies. Without `--json` serve lists no library, so it emits no `drift`.

`drift`:

| Field | Type | Meaning |
|---|---|---|
| `instance_id` | string | this serve process's instance id |
| `scan_counter` | integer | the counter of the snapshot the change is in, which this event follows |
| `name` | string | the skill's library name |
| `kind` | string | its kind now, `managed` or `unmanaged`, as the snapshot's `library` entry has it |
| `state` | string | its `state` now; absent when it has none, as an unmanaged skill has none |
| `drift` | array of strings | its `drift` now, sorted; `[]` when it is in none |
| `previous_state` | string | its `state` in the snapshot before; absent when it had none |
| `previous_drift` | array of strings | its `drift` in the snapshot before, sorted; `[]` when it was in none |

Update check: serve runs the update check of `agentx skill check-updates` (see **Checking for updates** under [Skills](#skills)) once its initial snapshot is out, and then every thirty minutes, or every `AGENTX_CHECK_INTERVAL`. The check runs off the loop that scans, so a rescan never waits for its network, and a tick that finds the last check still running is skipped rather than queued. It is the same check: the same comparison, the same candidates and markers written in one mutation with `last_fetched` and one bump of `version`, which sets off the rescan that brings them into the next snapshot's `library`. It covers the account remote as that check does (see **What your other machines published** under [Skills](#skills)): a tick fetches it whenever one of your own skills is placed here, announces what another machine published to them as `update_available`, writes its `last_fetched` and surfaces a fetch of it that fails once, as for a source. It differs in four ways. It refreshes every source: it fetches every source of the machine settings, not only the sources a managed skill came from, so that a source added to browse, or one nothing has been installed from yet, is fetched on every tick in the same pass, and each source is fetched once per tick whether or not a skill was installed from it. Each is the fetch of `source fetch` and no other (see **Fetch again** under [Sources](#sources)): the blobless fetch onto the staging ref, the `SKILL.md` blobs in one batch, then the source ref, in the user's environment with prompts disabled, as every git of serve is, at most four sources at a time and outside the lock. The one write at the end records `last_fetched`, now, for every source that fetched, beside the candidates and markers, and its bump of `version` makes serve rescan and rebuild its source index, so a search answered after a tick that moved a source ref finds the skills that source holds now. A tick runs whenever the settings hold a source, whether or not a managed skill came from one, a machine whose settings hold none spawning no git on any tick, and the git processes of a tick grow with its sources and not with the skills. A source that `agentx import` wrote into the settings is fetched only once `agentx source add <url>` has added it, and until then every tick reports it as the failure `source fetch` gives it, `source not fetched: <url>`, surfaced once as below. A machine that has no account repo yet, whose settings hold sources because the import wrote them, has nothing to fetch them into, so its ticks run no git and write nothing. Creating the account repo does not bring the others: the first `source add` makes the ticks fetch the source it added, and each other source the import wrote is reported the same way, at level `debug` after the first, until it is added too. It waits for the lock rather than giving up on it, for both of its holds, since it runs in the background and a command holding the lock for a moment is no reason to drop what it fetched; the holds are the short one before the network and the one write at the end, and every other command still gives up on a lock serve holds, with exit code 7, as it does on any other. It emits no `progress`. And it never ends serve: each check emits one `update_available` per skill it could check that holds a candidate, carrying `instance_id`, and what it could not check is logged. A source it could not fetch is logged on every check, at level `debug` on the checks that do not surface it, but surfaced once, so that a machine that is offline is not warned about the same source on every tick: a `log` warning prefixed `update check: ` from the first check that fails to fetch it, naming the source, the reason and the skills it left unchecked, `<message>; not checked: <name>, ...`, or the message alone for a source no managed skill came from, the message being the one `source fetch` gives the same failure; another warning only from a check that fails to fetch it for another cause, the cause being that message as [Streams](#streams) sanitises it, less what git reports differently on every attempt with the same cause: the time a connection took to fail, the bytes that came before a transfer stalled and the address of the host that hung up; and a `log` note at level `info`, `update check: <url> can be fetched again`, from the first check that fetches it again. Every check in between logs the same line at level `debug`, which only `--verbose` writes. What serve remembers of a failing source is kept in the memory of that serve process alone, so a new serve warns once again, and a source that leaves the settings is forgotten without a note; one removed while the check fetched it is not warned about at all. A skill whose newer version the check refuses to import, and a source it fetched but could not read, are a `log` warning prefixed `update check: ` on every check they fail. Every other warning of a check carries the same prefix: the notes a check that moves a candidate gives (see **Checking for updates**), which leave nothing unchecked, and a failure that ends the whole check, such as an account repo git cannot read, after which that check announces no update. A skill it could not check keeps its candidate, which the snapshot's `library` still carries.

Maintenance: serve maintains the account repo once a day, off the loop that scans, as the update check runs, and beside it: once its initial snapshot is out and then every hour, each time only when `maintained` in agentx home is missing or names a time 24 hours ago or more, or one in the future. It runs `git maintenance run --task=loose-objects` in the isolated environment, then `git maintenance run --task=incremental-repack` when the account repo holds a pack, which a new one does not, then, under the exclusive lock, waited for and with no change signal, since it takes the repository's `packed-refs.lock`, `git maintenance run --task=pack-refs`, or `git pack-refs --all` on a git that does not know that task, and writes `maintained`. None of them drops an object, reachable or not. `git maintenance start` is never run: it would install a job of its own with the system's scheduler and fetch on a timer of its own. A machine with no account repo runs no git for it. Serve stopping stops a maintenance git with SIGTERM, on which git removes its lock files, and the run is due again at the next start. Before a run, `objects/maintenance.lock` and `packed-refs.lock` of the account repo are removed under the exclusive lock when they are an hour old or more: a git killed outright left them, and git would skip every later maintenance and fail every ref deletion while they stay. A run that finds `objects/maintenance.lock` younger than that, another serve's maintenance, runs no git and does not write `maintained`, so it is due again at the next hourly ask; it is a `debug` line. A failure is a `log` warning prefixed `maintenance: `, and the same failure on a later run a `debug` line.

Scans: every scan holds the shared `flock` on `lock` over its local reads, the lineage its snapshot's `library` carries included, like `agentx scan`, but waits for a mutation in progress for as long as it takes instead of giving up after a second; it takes the exclusive lock only to recover an unfinished [mutation journal](#mutation-journal), and the update check above takes it for its own holds. Scans are serialised, one at a time. A change signal that arrives during a scan schedules one more scan after it.

Change signals: serve watches, in this order, agentx home (where every mutating command rewrites `version` last), `account.git`, `worktrees`, the library, the reflogs of the fork branches, `account.git/logs/refs/heads/skills`, the reflogs of the remote-tracking branches, `account.git/logs/refs/remotes`, and every user-scope skills directory a registered client reads, and then every directory below each of those trees, so an edit inside a skill is a signal too, wherever the scan reads that skill from. A commit made with git in a fork's worktree writes nothing in the worktree, agentx home or the library, only in the account repo, where it appends to its branch's reflog, which is how serve learns of it, and a publish with nothing to record moves only the remote-tracking branch, whose reflog is how serve learns that the fork's tip is published; the worktrees' own admin directories are not watched, since git writes an index there on every `git status`. The skills directories come from the whole registry, not from the detected clients alone, so a client whose configuration appears while serve runs is covered by the sync that first sees its directory; a directory no detected client reads costs a rescan that finds the inventory unchanged and emits nothing. Directories are watched on their real paths after resolving symlinks, and a real path reached twice, as a library symlink to a fork worktree is, as the library itself is for every client that reads it directly, and as the Claude Code directory is for Cursor, is watched once. Hidden directories and `node_modules` are skipped, as in discovery. Before every rescan the watches are brought in line with the directories that exist then: one that appeared, or did not exist at start, is watched from then on and one that disappeared is not. How the watching is done depends on the platform. On macOS, built with cgo as the release binary is, one FSEvents stream covers every watched directory and everything below the trees, at no cost per file, plus every directory a symlink below a tree leads out of it, since FSEvents does not follow symlinks; a change is a signal when it happens in a directory the rules above cover. Everywhere else, and on macOS built without cgo, each directory gets its own non-recursive watch: one inotify watch on Linux, where a library of a few hundred skills and the client directories beside it stay within the default limit, and on macOS without cgo one kqueue descriptor per directory and per file in it, which meets the default limit of 256 open files early. A placement that is a symlink into the library resolves to a path already watched and costs nothing; a placement that is a copy is watched on its own. Events are debounced: a rescan runs 100 ms after the last event, or 500 ms after the first event of a burst, whichever comes first. Watching is a precondition of serve: when the watcher cannot be created, or a directory cannot be watched at start or later, serve ends with exit code 6 and an `error` event whose message names the directory and the cause, after any snapshot already emitted; the hint names the limit to raise. Serve never rescans on a timer in place of watching.

Stdin carries requests, one JSON object per line: `refresh` and `search`. A line that is not a JSON object, a request without a known `type`, a refresh without a `request_id`, or a search without a `request_id` or without a `query` is answered with an `error` event with code `usage` on stdout, and serve keeps running; under serve an `error` event therefore does not announce the end of the run. Blank lines are ignored.

`{"type": "refresh", "request_id": "<unique id>"}` asks for a scan. A refresh is satisfied only by a scan that begins after the request was received; a scan already in progress cannot satisfy it, and every request received before the next scan begins shares that scan. After the scan, a changed snapshot is emitted first, then one `refresh_complete` per request.

`refresh_complete`:

| Field | Type | Meaning |
|---|---|---|
| `request_id` | string | the id from the request |
| `instance_id` | string | this serve process's instance id |
| `ok` | boolean | whether the scan succeeded |
| `scan_counter` | integer | the counter of the current snapshot, changed or not; absent when `ok` is `false`, since a failed scan claims no freshness |
| `error` | string | why the scan failed; present only when `ok` is `false` |

A failed rescan is also reported as a `log` warning; the last snapshot stays in force and serve keeps running. A failed initial scan ends serve with the error's exit code.

`{"type": "search", "request_id": "<unique id>", "query": "<text>"}` searches the skills of every source in [machine settings](#machine-settings), so that the app never filters a listing itself. It is answered with one `search` event:

| Field | Type | Meaning |
|---|---|---|
| `request_id` | string | the id from the request |
| `instance_id` | string | this serve process's instance id |
| `query` | string | the text from the request |
| `results` | array | one object per matching skill: `source`, `subpath`, `name`, `description` and `tree`; empty when nothing matched |

A skill matches when its name or its description contains the query, compared case-insensitively. Results are sorted by source canonical URL, then name, then subpath. `source` is the canonical URL rather than the source id that `source_skill` carries: a search spans every source, and the URL is what names one to a person and what an install takes. `subpath`, `name`, `description` and `tree` are the skill as `source skills` lists it, `subpath` being `""` for a skill at the repository root.

Serve answers from the source index it holds in memory: one skill list per source in the settings, built from that source's ref `refs/agentx/sources/<id>` in the account repo exactly as `agentx source skills` builds its listing, over the whole source rather than a subpath, since a subpath scopes one listing and is never stored. The index is built after the initial scan and rebuilt after every later one, each time after that scan's snapshot has gone out and before its `refresh_complete` events, so that a snapshot never waits for it. Building it reads the settings under the shared `lock`, as a scan does, and reads the sources and the commits their refs hold in one `for-each-ref`; a source still at the commit the last build read it at keeps that build's skills rather than being listed again, so when every source is, and none has gone or arrived, the index stands unchanged and nothing else runs, and rebuilding it after a rescan that follows a change elsewhere on the machine costs one git process, and none on a machine with no source. A source the last build could not list is listed again whatever its commit: that failure is the account repo's answer at that moment, not a property of the commit, so an index built while a source was momentarily unreadable recovers on the next build instead of holding that source empty until its ref moves. This is how a source added, fetched again or removed reaches the index: each of those is a mutation of agentx home, whose change signal schedules the scan, and a refresh acknowledged after it is answered from the new index. The update check above is one too: it fetches every source on its timer and ends in one write, so the index follows what the sources hold while serve runs, a source no skill was installed from included, without a refresh being asked for. A source whose ref the account repo does not hold, and one it cannot list whole, contribute no skills and are a `log` warning each. The first is warned about by the build that first finds it so and not by the builds after it that keep that reading, so a rebuild that follows an update check moving another source's ref does not repeat the warning; one that leaves the settings and comes back still not fetched, or whose ref goes again after a fetch, is warned about anew. The second is warned about by every build, since each lists it again.

A search takes no lock, runs no git process and makes no network call. It is answered from the index as it stands, while a scan is in progress, without waiting for it, so a search sent after a refresh can be answered before that refresh's `refresh_complete`.

`agentx serve --once` takes the serve lock, runs the initial scan, emits its snapshot and exits 0 with exactly the events `snapshot` and `result`. It reconciles nothing, watches nothing, reads no requests and runs no update check and no maintenance, and with no snapshot before its one it emits no `drift`.
